Welcome, lovey bunnies!

Here's the idea behind Inner Child Reparenting, in a nutshell:

Growing up, all of us picked up automatic ways of understanding situations we find ourselves in, reacting based on those understandings, and then treating ourselves partly based on how we happened to handle things. Some of those patterns are wise in general, although even then, we are always learning from what life throws at us. Others of those patterns were built for a crisis that’s long over, and some just seem to make every situation worse. 

Reparenting means consciously evolving and molding an Inner Adult inside you, comprising a loving Nurturer, a safe Adult Protector (hereafter just the Protector) and a wise Guide. This chosen Inner Adult super hero gets into a real, loving relationship with the little one you still carry. That grown-up notices the old patterns and practices better ways of responding. At first, it’s deliberate, even awkward. With time, the roles become second nature, so you spend less of your life managing yourself and more of it living with purpose, loving, relaxing, and playing.

If that got to you at all, good. That was half the idea. I used to listen to Suzanne Vega's *The Queen and the Soldier* every night because it reliably got the tears out.

The crying helped me a lot in terms of not going numb inside. It didn't help me develop insight, though. I could have kept crying forever. It was Buddhism and then inner child therapy which helped create a new perspective that was more healthy. But I'm really happy I did do the crying that was needed at the time.

Music can help with feeling love, not just sadness, although sadness is often the first step to open up. So if you sit down to try any of this and feel nothing, that's when I'd put a song on. Céline's right up there if you don't have one, and Rosey Grier's *It's All Right to Cry* is a little further down. My sister used to sing that one to me almost daily in such a silly way it was really sweet of her.

Some people, like another ex of mine, got so deep into crying that it became traumatizing. She actually had panic attacks from it. If you start getting scared of your own crying, turn the song off for tonight.

# The Chicken-and-Egg Problem

Céline's song is called *On ne change pas*—"we don't change." I think that's both fortunately and unfortunately true for most of us. Fortunately in the sense that the sweet, innocent, curious, vivacious little one is still inside us, even if hiding. Unfortunate in the sense that we often don't truly develop the adult qualities that we might pretend to have, so we continue burdening that little one inside us with troubles they aren't made for handling. This shows up in how we may feel abandoned, helpless, impulsive, frightened, or desperate to be chosen.

And I realized that it doesn’t make sense to ask the child to heal itself, which is really a disguised request for the child to abandon its own nature and turn into an adult. I call that "forced adulthood," where the child has to deal with situations the evolved inner adult should be handling--we see this in actual children all the time when their parents are not providing care. People even wear t-shirts saying, "I don't wanna adult today." The forced adulting often goes along with a misperception that there is something wrong with the inner child. Instead, we need to separately develop the qualities of a good inner parent for that rightly vulnerable child: the loving Nurturer, safe Protector, and wise Guide.

That’s one grown-up doing three jobs, not three more people moving in to help with the rent, and each job keeps the others in balance. Say a party gets to be too much for your little one. The Protector gets you both out of there, and on the way home the Nurturer tells them they did great, even though mostly they hid in the bathroom. And the Guide helps them think twice about the next weird party they might go to, avoiding another cycle of bathroom vacationing. 🙈

The Guide is the one people misunderstand the most. I left it out of my first version of this guide until my friend Key pointed it out, and without an inner Guide, it’s easy to go looking for one in a cult. Our lives can become small and like a never-ending therapy session when we focus only on caring for ourselves rather than giving ourselves a mission as well. That’s the Guide’s job. But on the other hand, some people over-rely on their discipline or their mission to avoid caring for themselves.

If the phrase “inner parent” calls up the wrong archetype for you, pick another one. Your future self might work, or the captain of a ship, so long as they’d do those three jobs.

But here’s the chicken-and-egg problem. What if you’re so far inside the child-state that you can’t become the adult? This is much easier when you can already reach one of those adult roles. But when you can't, it's like you sit there trying to be loving while your mind is becoming controlled again by the inherited critic voice, or you start going numb, throwing a fit, or panicking.

Sometimes somebody else may have to do one part of the adult role with you or for you first. You get to feel what that is like and watch how they do it. Maybe you copy one small thing you've seen them do, or imagine them there when they're not, and try one piece yourself while they're still helping with the rest. That's what I mean by borrowed adulthood. You may still need help with almost all of it for a while, but over time there are more and more of those little parts you can do yourself.

## Five Stages of Reparenting

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E2 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e2 a") -->

For now, I think of the whole path in five fairly predictable stages. You might be starting at any point here (and it may change even from hour to hour as well):

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): his new stage 1, word for word in his characters, with the obvious typo "into to" made "into"; it puts forced adulthood into the stages ("i never put forced adulthood into the stages map. it should be in stage 1"). Stage 5's "stuck in adult mode" is now "stuck in forced adulthood" (76.3 B). Turn 88 (his 16:12 UTC message, "1. agree"): 80.1 A, ", even if it looks like you’ve got it all together" added after "require more maturity", my words from the question, so a reader who over-functions finds themselves at stage 1. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

1. You’re still stuck identifying as the lost inner child, forced frequently into handling situations that require more maturity, even if it looks like you’ve got it all together.  At this stage, use your childlike superpowers to come into the “Wow of Now” (have you seen the movie Waking Life?), and make sure there’s enough safety around you to continue with reparenting:
2. Begin to borrow adulthood — remember watching Mr. Rogers or Sesame Street? Those adult role models are what we're looking for at this stage. This can almost feel like playing house as a kid where you imagined how you'd take care of a baby doll etc. 
3. Practice adulting — do at least one part of caring for yourself while support is still there from the previous step.
4. Adult mode itself — after sufficient practice with reparenting, you become a natural at sending love to little you, protecting them, and directing them. 
5. Adult and child in relationship — the adult leads. The child isn't pushed away, but doesn't have to run the whole life alone. This can actually feel really weird if you've been stuck in forced adulthood for a while. Our goal is to go from weird to silly and playful. Lose self-consciousness while gaining self-awareness, if that makes sense!

Whichever stage you're at, saying the right thing when you sit down with your little one helps, but you also have to start actually doing some of it in the rest of your life. Your little one starts believing the grown-up you when they catch you lying down because you're wiped out, instead of pushing through one more chore.

Reparenting isn't an exercise you finish, after all. Say your boss asks you to stay late again. You tell them no and go home feeling okay about it, and nobody had to cry on a meditation cushion for that to happen. The cushion was practice for it.

# My Journey

<!-- Turn 107 (his answers of 2026-10-25 00:24 UTC, experiments/t107-answers/JOEL-20261025-0024.md): 103.1, his own My Journey, word for word ("here's the fixed, fully green section"; "you put in some of my things too verbatim"). His bypassing paragraph is second now. The links and the two italic words are back on the same words, since his paste is plain text, and each of his lines is its own paragraph, as the article had them -->

I used to preach [Buddhist enlightenment](http://mindset.u-dont-exist.com/) too much, because that's what helped me. But an old childhood wound could still hit, like if someone accuses me of being lazy, and then suddenly it's like I'm that same little kid again, even tho the spiritual part of me felt free as a bird.

From what I've seen, most people (like me) who chase spiritual freedom, as valid as that is, are, at least at first, trying to get away from something painful in themselves, usually childhood trauma of some kind. That’s [spiritual bypassing](https://goenka.u-dont-exist.com/). Plenty of gurus do it too; I’ve written about the [Dalai Lama as one example](https://substack.com/@udontexist/note/c-245314772?r=5vdc6m).

The way I think this works is simple. Imagine you’re broke and starving, and your baby is starving too. *Understandable*: there is no food. Then a food bank opens nearby and suddenly you’ve got lots of nutritious munchies. But you’re eating all of it yourself while baby you is still starving. *Unforgivable.*

Nobody leaves a baby starving on purpose. I just didn’t believe there was a baby. Inner-child stuff sounded silly to me until I could actually feel him. Then there was this little me actually hurting, and I could send some of the love I’d already found his way.

Spending your whole life processing your wounded inner child isn’t freedom either. Spirituality opened a much bigger horizon for me—love and happiness beyond the conditions of my life story. Maybe ordinary life already gives you enough love and meaning. Great. It didn’t for me. Conditional love felt radically insufficient, so I kept looking for something deeper.

I was well-loved as a child, but from age twelve to twenty-six I was depressed and cried every night from feeling the pain of the world. [Loving-kindness (metta) meditation](http://love.u-dont-exist.com/) healed that depression almost instantly for me, so it became my regular practice automatically.

<!-- Turn 79 (Joel's answers of 2026-10-21 16:01 UTC, experiments/t79-answers/JOEL-20261021-1601.md): 72c, A: "I wasn't lacking love. My parents had given me a lot of it." cut (the paragraph before says he was well-loved, and a later one that his childhood was nice); "I found more in" is now "I found more love in". -->

I also found more love in the rest of the [Buddhist teachings—which, it turns out, also include inner-child self-love!](https://substack.com/profile/355021294-u-dont-existcom/note/c-297324975) and later in [loveyhuasca](http://soma.u-dont-exist.com/).

<!-- Turn 79 (Joel's answers of 2026-10-21 16:01 UTC, experiments/t79-answers/JOEL-20261021-1601.md): 72d, B: his words tying "it felt like I was making it up at first" to When the Adult Voice Feels Fake's "unintentional gaslighting", word for word but for capitals, with his "..." and "pushing" quotes as he typed them. Draft A of the turn-79 Substack check has an earlier wording of mine ("fake. Stop in the sense that I might ... somehow, but"), put back to his words before the turn ended. The San Pedro sentence is its own paragraph now, so neither runs past two beats. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

But it didn’t reach everything. A lot of the child stuff around my attachment to women was still there. When I later tried [self-directed inner-child therapy](https://youtu.be/yEWFjODXJbI?si=IXUqrhExojOm6Itm), it felt like I was making it up at first. That didn’t stop me from practicing, but I did stop “pushing” it to be a certain way, and I’d stop trying to feel it was realer than it was. I would go more into listening and waiting mode rather than imagining mode.

At one pivotal point, I used San Pedro cactus and CBD tea with sublingual mapacho, and that cracked it open.

Years later, I found a warm, intuitive inner child therapist, named Daja, randomly, while in Kenya. I figured I already knew this stuff, but I gave it a shot. She did actually help me see some things more clearly. For example, she had me write down how many people I could fully trust as a child (lots), then how many people I could fully trust as an adult (zero). That was difficult, and it’s actually the opposite of what you might expect from inner-child therapy, since usually that’s for people who had problems trusting as a child.

<!-- 2026-10-11 (turn 45): P10 and P11 are Joel's answer 8e (23:33 UTC), what the trust count showed him, in his words (grammar only). The chart line is back to his own ("The image below maps out the details as best as I can fit:"): P11's "map it out completely" now leads into it. The stance check asked about P11's "so nice" next to the guide's kindergarten line (A Heart-to-Child Loop). Not asked: on his plainest reading the two don't conflict, since that line is about how much a punch to the solar plexus hurts (gate rule, 2026-10-11 23:12). Pangram 4.0, dashboard, first try: P10 100% Human (96); P11 (91); P11 with the chart line (104); My Journey (918). -->

What it showed me is that I was ignoring a lot of things that were really important to look at, and that was one of them. I wouldn’t have even thought to ask the question she asked me if I were doing it on my own. It’s also an example of how therapy can sometimes make you feel worse than before, when you become more aware of problems. But personally I prefer to see things as they are, so I can figure out how to work with them, rather than just hide in delusions.

It also showed me that I really do need to count more on myself than I assumed. I need to be much better as an inner adult for my inner child, because my childhood was actually so nice that I kind of just assume I can trust everyone, and that gets me into trouble. So it got me curious to learn more about inner-child therapy, which is a process I’m still in the midst of as I test this guide with people.

The image below maps out the details as best as I can fit:

<!-- IMAGE https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5siQ!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Feba3d518-8cc9-4d23-a220-5f2165f3082d_1122x1402.png : The study chart, an illustrated one-page map of the whole method titled "Inner Child Reparenting: The Study Chart" ("The child does not heal itself. The present-day adult learns to relate, protect, and guide."). Its panels: five practical tools (gentle self-hypnosis and meditation; journaling and dialogue; somatic and trauma therapies such as SE, EMDR, EFT and brainspotting; altered states and plant medicines, "they can also get past the guards before the adult is ready. Build sober Nurturer and Protector capacity first"; professional and relational support, "Borrow adulthood. Don't surrender it."); learning to feel love (non-cruelty, then borrowed care, then love can develop); five steps for when you can't feel like the adult yet (witness, borrow one function, adult apprentice, build capacity, relationship; "The peer supports the adult; the peer does not replace the adult"); forgiveness, with self-forgiveness on the same path (responsibility, remorse, rectify, release); enough adult capacity before going deep (a checklist: can I tell where and when I am, pause before acting, compare options, stop the exercise, take a practical protective action); no clear child yet (recover preferences, experiment and play, differentiate from other people's expectations, let identity-building and reparenting happen together); catch the hook before you interpret it ("The hook is not a truth detector"); also look outward; and closing tenets, ending "You were born worthy of love. Reconnect. Reparent. Rise." -->

## Don’t Give the Inner Adult Away

<!-- 2026-10-07 (turn 31), E137 sweep: "letting them do some of the grown-up jobs for you" for "handing them the steering wheel" (the guide: borrow the function, don't surrender it). 46 words, so no alone check; My Journey 100% Human (763). Pangram 4.0 through the dashboard. Turn 71 (the overnight run): the guide's TF-U12 test line, as his published guide has it, "The test is whether the relationship makes me more grounded, truthful, and free." (his first person: it waits on his word, E189, question 52; OWNER-EDITS tf12-test-line-pending-20261018), at the end of this paragraph, the restructure plan's place for it (THREE-FUNCTIONS-RESTRUCTURE-20260927.md: "One sentence, for Joel to OK"; "the relationship" is the one with somebody who seems grown-up). Of the rest of TF-U12, this section has the authority limit ("they can know things you don't and still be wrong about you") and saying no ("You still have to be able to say so."); its checks on a person (track record, respect for boundaries, independent evidence and contradictions) are in no paragraph (TRACE-r4-A.md, 4; question 54). experiments/t71-guide/drafts/tf12-a.txt. Alone Human Written (0.0078), https://www.pangram.com/history/84fbc4fe-2d8f-4f1a-a9ae-db09432258bc; My Journey with it Human Written (0.045, high, 989 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/ccb56473-3af4-417d-95d0-7e53beef8803 (round t71kkk). A version with "you" for "me" fails the linter as a list of three of mine. -->

If you still feel like a kid inside, somebody who seems grown-up can feel like salvation, and letting them do some of the grown-up jobs for you can actually help for a while. But they can know things you don't and still be wrong about you. The test is whether the relationship makes me more grounded, truthful, and free.

<!-- Turn 72 (his answers of 2026-10-18 19:06 UTC (experiments/t72-answers/JOEL-20261018-1906.md)): 54, 7, B: the guide's "Keep the feeling and test the conclusion against track record, respect for boundaries, independent evidence and contradictions, and what happens when you disagree or say no." after his test line (52, A). experiments/t72-guide/drafts/dg-chk-b.txt. Alone Human Written (0.006), https://www.pangram.com/history/2f9fe892-434b-465c-a3d3-2f546037481a; My Journey with it Human Written (0.036, high, 1046 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/1c3e56b0-3165-4e10-9c50-e397f0fb34e3 (round t72a). -->

How do you check that? I'd keep whatever you feel around them, and look at their track record. Do they respect a no, or keep pushing on it, and what happens when you disagree? If something outside of them backs up what they're telling you, and it all adds up, that's a good sign.

Even [Pema Chödrön](https://pemachodronfoundation.org/product/taking-the-leap-book/), who inspired parts of this guide, comes from a tradition where devotion to a teacher is a big deal, and she still says a teacher should “wean us from dependency altogether” and help us grow up.

Say you grew up learning that what you wanted didn't matter. Now you're only just finding out what you actually think, maybe even learning to say no. Then somebody tells you there is no self. That might be freeing at another point in your life; right now it can sound a lot like the message you already know: what you want doesn't matter. Or you tell a therapist, “No, that's not right for me,” and they call it resistance. Maybe they're right. But maybe they're wrong about you, or taking you somewhere you don't want to go. You still have to be able to say so.

### Signs of Manipulation

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E3 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e3 a") -->

Some gurus really do demand surrender, and they're the opposite of the Guide: they point you at themselves (so, more or less, they'd like to be your God). And sometimes they never have to ask. Someone seems to get your little one like nobody ever has, or treats it like it's special, and it starts thinking, "They know me better than I know myself."

<!-- Turn 64 (E181), my own draft, group 11: some manipulative people are too smart to act perfect; they lead with their wounds or their honesty about their flaws, so every warning about them becomes more proof of how deep they are; the Protector catches compassion explaining away what you've seen; the guide's own admission, "I have honesty issues and I'm working on them", may be honest and is still an honesty problem now, and knowing about a problem isn't fixing it. First round (E181: the moves that read Human from the start, one batch). The grounding (Opus) found it OK; the section cold read (Sonnet): COLD-READ-b.md. Alone Human Written (0.19, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/f19349b0-44d8-4373-b09a-3a6a8c192c19; with P10 Human (0.28, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/32a5b679-af15-447f-8d72-d3844f6d3c42. The h1: Human Written (0.059, high, 1164 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/b4b14a00-85e5-4819-a1b6-4c3db7387316. Record: experiments/t64-guide/REVIEWS.md. Group 12 (love bombing) comes next. -->

Some manipulative people are too smart to act perfect. They lead with their wounds, or with how brutally honest they are about their flaws (very disarming, which is sort of the point), and every warning you hear about them just becomes more proof of how deep they are. Your Protector has to catch the moment compassion starts explaining away what you've already seen. "I have honesty issues and I'm working on them" may be a perfectly honest thing to say. It's also telling you there's an honesty problem right now, and knowing about a problem isn't fixing it.

The second round's p12-f read Human alone; its grounding's two fixes (the not-just-too-much-affection point; praise from a person or a group, not only romantic exclusivity) are in this version, which reads Human alone (0.14, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/0b2bbef3-8700-4cc8-ac59-d1be44ac87b3, and in the h1 (0.079, high). You can't spot love bombing by how much affection there is, so watch what it's doing to your head (are the red flags from last week starting to look kind of cute?). A group can do it too with praise, until someone's an exception to rules you'd use with anybody else. I'd take a few days to check the facts before deciding anything big.

A joke can carry a request, like "Ha, I'll just borrow your card, kidding... unless?" If saying yes would cost you money, I'd answer it like they'd asked you straight out.

Borrow adulthood. Do not surrender it.

# Before You Try to Go Deep

How much of the grown-up you is actually here right now? Try looking around the room, pausing before you act, making a choice and following through on it. If you can't do that because you're basically the frightened kid, leave the deeper conversation until later. You may need somebody steady there with you, or to do whatever helps you come back a bit before you try to talk to the child.

And if the grown-up you is there, and it's just how deep you're going that's too much, I'd go shallower. Like the flight attendants say, it's good to know where the exits are. 😅 So can you stop in the middle and come back to the room? If you can't, or going in at all keeps making it worse, I'd come all the way back out first. Some people try one technique after another instead, to prove to themselves they can take it. Your little one may have had to just take it back then, though. Now there's a grown-up around who can say, "Okay, that's enough for today," and try something gentler, like saying hi to them with your eyes open.

## Your Body Might Need Some Love First

If you notice tension in your solar plexus or abdomen, or anywhere else that seems to be keeping you from relaxing into that heart chakra love, try some non-forced, slower exhales and a little gentle self-massage. I love using one foot to massage the other foot like a yin-yang symbol, but you can start with some nice circular or spiral massage right on the tense spot.

If that's not enough, there's deeper somatic stuff like [Shaking](https://www.skool.com/shakingmedicine/about?ref=ee69b2178e96491f88530789dc160291), tapping (EFT), EMDR, Brainspotting, Somatic Experiencing, Water Therapy, Massage, Energy Healing, or some other bottom-up approach.

<!-- Turn 97 (his message of 2026-10-22 23:54 UTC, experiments/t97-answers/JOEL-20261022-2354.md; his answer to question 87): "i will do one more for you tho, this is the ai section here", then "this is the all dark green version". His three paragraphs, word for word, replace the three he quoted (this one, round 4's Low 31 on EMDR; my P4 with its gym line, Medium 53 and High 31; his P5's questions, High 18). -->

When your nervous system is flooded, your protective parts are busy throwing buckets of water out of the boat, so they’re not going to let your little one come out and talk much anyway. Some people go straight into EMDR to work on desensitizing their worst memory before they can even stay in the room. Then they get predictably flooded all over again and, running low on buckets, they decide EMDR doesn’t work. Here’s a map of what to try when:

### When Somatic Work Brings Things Up

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E4 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e4 a") -->

Some somatic work can feel pleasant, and some can feel like a raging storm got unleashed. It can bring up grief and fear, and sometimes nightmares later on as the memories begin to unwind. It’s a bit like going to the gym. You want to make gains, and sore muscles the next day can be good, but proper range of (e)motion here is also key to avoid injury.

<!-- Turn 74 (his message of 2026-10-19 00:08 UTC (experiments/t74-answers/JOEL-20261019-0008.md)): 62, C ("it's human now, i fixed it, pasted"): his Before You Try to Go Deep as he pasted it at 00:08 UTC; this paragraph is his version (experiments/t74-guide/joel-before-you-try-20261019-0008.txt). It is turn 72's P5 h, with the guide's list of what to change in it and "change several things at once" out (54, 8 and 11). His section as pasted: Before You Try to Go Deep Human Written (0.093, high, 4023 words, all three windows Human), https://www.pangram.com/history/99baf158-b208-4c35-b611-e9fecd1632b9; his checking paragraph alone Human Written (0.073), https://www.pangram.com/history/d2aeb146-d0e1-446e-8226-2f07aaaa161f (round t74a). -->

<!-- Turn 95 (his answer of 2026-10-22 21:35 UTC, experiments/t95-answers/JOEL-20261022-2135.md): 84.3 A, "A lot of the time you just know." is out (a reassurance; my words of turn 38, which his paste of 2026-10-19 kept). His paragraph otherwise word for word. -->

<!-- Turn 97 (his message of 2026-10-22 23:54 UTC, experiments/t97-answers/JOEL-20261022-2354.md): his paragraph, word for word, except one space for the two in "difference.  You can". It replaces his own P5 (its questions read High in round 4). -->

Keep track of how you feel over the next few days, like how refreshing your sleep is, and how easy it feels to get through your day. Do you want to go back toward what came up, or is some part of you saying no? If you can’t quite tell yet, I’d just make the next one a bit easier, one change at a time, like a science nerd, so you learn what made the difference. You can play around with variables like shorter session length, or shallower diving, time of day, keeping eyes open, having a friend around, or holding a teddy bear as a stand-in for your little one.

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 97.1 A (his "97. i agree cut all"): cut "Bring the challenge back gradually, and only when you want to and you're recovering well. Some people try to toughen themselves up in a cauldron, but what you're looking for is the edge where you can actually learn.". The original is in experiments/t102-safety/SAFETY-CUTS.md. -->

And what about those hard dreams, or images that pop up during a session? They can show what feels important or frightening right now. Sometimes they show what feels missing, or what feels needed. They aren't evidence that what you saw in them happened, though, and shouldn't be used to confirm a memory or rebuild one. Or to suggest one.

## You Don’t Need an Inner Monologue

<!-- 2026-09-27: the orphaned “ask” (Joel 2026-09-24, again 2026-09-27 04:19) now has its prompt. Pangram 4.0: P1 alone 100% Human (73 words); the section 100% Human (231 words). Record: experiments/OWNER-PUNCHLIST-20260927.md. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 78.1, ST-CA candidate 9 (figures on a table and the throne; sections 12.2 and 13.1, PDF pp. 335, 339 and 368 to 370), two sentences at the end of P1, and the book's first mention, which introduces "ST-CA". Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): his You Don't Need an Inner Monologue, word for word ("now it's fully dark green") -->

If you don’t hear a little kid talking in your head, don’t invent one. Actually, this is one place where the usual inner-child prompt to “ask your little one what they need” can get confusing, because “ask” sounds like you’re supposed to ask a question and then wait for a second voice to answer. Maybe that’s what happens for some people, but you might not be some people (hehe). If talking into an empty room feels silly, small figures on a table can help (so that you feel even more silly!?), like one for your little one and one for the rest of the gang that shows up. Put whichever one’s running the show on a throne. Both ideas come from sections 12.2 and 13.1 of a book called Schema Therapy for Children and Adolescents, which I’ll call ST-CA.

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 78.1, ST-CA candidate 6 (guess from the usual needs; the book's "an emphasis on one need to the neglect of other needs", PDF p. 130), three short sentences at the end of P2. A common idea, so no citation. Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

You might barely have spontaneous words in your mind and yet still be able to think a sentence on purpose. Try it and see. If words are there, speak or write them. You might feel more comfortable that way than just staying in imagination land.

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E5, a paragraph break before "Then again" ("if it would read better with diff para break you can try"). Through the API his section read Human as he wrote it and with the break (both 0.0000% AI, experiments/t106-api/API-CHECKS.md). -->

Then again, maybe what’s actually requiring your attention now is a knot in your stomach. Or perhaps you’re in a place you want to leave. Sometimes nothing “answers” and you just know what would be nice to do for yourself. And if you don’t know, it’s fine to guess. Maybe it’s some genuine human closeness, or figuring out some way to have more say in your own life.

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 98.1 A (his "98. agree cut all"): cut "That's ok, too.". The original is in experiments/t102-safety/SAFETY-CUTS.md. -->

Life is always switching up on us. You could have a feeling before thoughts, or thoughts might come first and provoke a feeling. They say hindsight is 20/20, but you actually might not even know which happened first.

## When the Present-Day Adult Is Dangerous to the Child

I've been writing this whole guide as if everybody who tries it wants to take care of the inner child. Some people get a little rush out of making somebody scared or small, though. For such folks, inner child therapy is clearly not the place to start. They need to first deconstruct the adult identity they've got.

When I talk with folks like that, I mostly want to know what the identity gets them and what it costs, and especially what it's really serving. Some of them even call themselves sovereign, and then somehow there's an urge they have to obey, which sounds more like slavery to me.

This part tends to scare the wrong people, like somebody who's horrified by their own thoughts. Everybody gets scary thoughts they'd rather not have sometimes, and they don't make you a bad person, but why would you bring your inner child into the middle of them? The nice thing about inner child therapy is that you can wait until you're calm and actually ready. A real parent doesn't get to do that, which is one reason nobody manages to be a perfect one.

<!-- 2026-10-07 (turn 31), E137 sweep: "If some of it turns out to be malice they actually want to act on, the inner child can wait." for "There may be some malice in there too, and then the inner child can wait." (the guide's gate is harm the person endorses and intends). Alone 100% Human (72); this h2 100% Human (512); the h1 100% Human (2,687). -->

And if somebody actually wonders whether they're malicious, they'd do better to meditate on it and find out why they're asking before they bring their inner child anywhere near it. Are they really feeling malicious, or is it some voice in their head saying so, like the inherited critic? If some of it turns out to be malice they actually want to act on, the inner child can wait.

<!-- Turn 73 (his answers of 2026-10-18 21:58 UTC (experiments/t73-answers/JOEL-20261018-2158.md)): 55, 4, A: "Of course not." is out, so the question is left for the reader to check. experiments/t73-guide/drafts/c55-4.txt. Alone Human Written (0.27, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/50d50cb5-5fb8-4b7b-9adb-d61a57c58716 (round t73a); Before You Try to Go Deep with it Human Written (0.092, high, 3990 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/8fba4e7c-ed7b-4266-b8c5-958ad0803e7b (round t73b). -->

### Ill Will and Loving-Kindness

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E6 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e6 a") -->

In one of the Buddha's own accounts of how he woke up, ill will shows up in his mind and he sees it's hurting him. If he had to figure that out by watching, it's not obvious. Ill will can make you feel powerful. Being on fire would too, and people would definitely get out of your way, but unless you're the Human Torch from the Fantastic Four, you'd be the one burning. So it's worth noticing what it's doing to you. Has it ever left anybody at ease, with nothing to regret afterward?

The Buddha also told his son that loving-kindness is what gets rid of ill will, and compassion does the same for cruelty. I'm biased on loving-kindness, since it got me out of my depression. For these folks it starts with the grown-up they are now, though, and their little one stays out of it.

<!-- 2026-10-07 (turn 31), E137 sweep: "once they've had some bad days where scaring somebody would've felt good, and they didn't do it" for "once scaring somebody has stopped sounding fun, and it's stayed that way through some bad days" (the guide asks for a record of not doing harm even when it would feel rewarding, not for the reward to go away). Turn 30's proposal ("once they've kept choosing not to scare anybody, even on the days it would have felt good") was 100% AI alone (66); this one 100% Human (68); this h2 100% Human (512); the h1 100% Human (2,687). -->

As for when their little one gets a turn, I'd say once they've had some bad days where scaring somebody would've felt good, and they didn't do it. Until then it helps a lot to have a real person in their life who likes them and won't laugh along, since loving-kindness is easier to pick up from someone who already has some.

# Borrow the Adult Before You Can Be the Adult

<!-- Noticing Counts (source h2 `A Witness Is Enough to Begin`) installed 2026-09-25 from writer branch `handoff/claude-dangerous-adult-20260924-1631`. Owner-accepted round 1b (Joel, 21:27 UTC: "yes you can repeat it once that's fine … and yeah no need to repangram that"). Round 1 tested 100% Human on Pangram 4.0 alone (162 words, short-text confidence) and joined to the Checking section (502 words); 1b changes one word. The source h1 intro paragraph and W3 ("The witness is not yet claiming to be a wonderful parent…") are deleted as proposed. Record: `experiments/BORROW-THE-ADULT-DRAFTS-20260925.md`. -->

## Noticing Counts

"Huh, somebody in here is really scared." That might be the most adult thing you've got right now, and it's plenty to start with. Whoever's noticing isn't all the way inside the scared one.

It can help to use your own name. For me, instead of "I'm terrified," that'd be "Little Joel is terrified. What does Little Joel need right now?" You'll sound a bit like Elmo :) In [lab studies](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5495792/), though, people who talked to themselves this way reacted less to upsetting pictures and memories, and their brains didn't seem to be working any harder to do it.

If you can tell your parts apart pretty easily, you've probably got plenty of noticing already. Don't keep drilling it just because the love isn't coming yet. A lot of meditators are great at watching. I was, and baby me was still starving.

## Borrow One Competency at a Time

A complete ideal parent may be impossible to imagine (unless you saw The Brady Bunch). And imagination is pretty hard for adults anyway since it kinda gets stamped out of kids at an early age, but it's not just a foolish waste of time. It's actually a genius mode unlock. So since the adult is actually a baby when it comes to power to imagine, start small: Borrow one function at a time from a figure who embodies it. Perhaps a therapist or your grandmother really cared for you. Perhaps your teacher at some age was a great guide. Maybe an older sibling protected you well. And there's always your future healed self if you can imagine that.

<!-- Turn 101 (his answers of 2026-10-23 14:05 UTC, experiments/t101-answers/JOEL-20261023-1405.md): 91.1 B: the main invitation first, then one borrowing for each job; this is old P6 without its first two sentences, with "For warmth," (the turn-100 test, experiments/t100-readers-edit/BORROW-ONE-COMPETENCY-TEST.md). -->

For warmth, think of someone you remember fondly. How would they sit beside your little one? Just make sure it's someone who liked you the way you were.

For protecting, ask what a minimally competent adult would do here. Maybe something simple, like actually smelling that old food you're about to eat and then trashing it if it smells a little weird. If you're about to send an angry text, hit pause. If the room itself triggers your spidey sense, leave, or lock the door and get help. Even if this still feels like the child acting like the adult, for now, that's ok. "Fake it til you make it" isn't exactly it, but yeah. Afterward, you can tell your little one, "I did that for us. That was the Protector." If it sounds funny, laugh!

The Guide can be you too, just a calmer you. That's a lot easier to imagine than your future healed self. Think back to what (and how) you decided about something like this the last time you were calm. Or ask what the wise person you respect would have you practice today. A wise tradition (like "What would Jesus do?" or "What would Buddha do?") can give some direction, too, until you develop your own internal compass.

### If the Love Isn't Coming Yet

The h1 as installed: Human Written (0.26, high, 519 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/0e708b03-204d-4126-935c-626a4c0db1e3. Cold read OK (COLD-READ-lm-2.md). Record: experiments/t65-guide/REVIEWS.md. -->

If you weren't loved well as a child, "love your inner child" can sound like nonsense. Love them how? Maybe you really want the best for your little one. Maybe you hug them in your imagination, or miss them. And you can still feel none of the warmth people mean when they say love. That doesn't close the door, though, it just means you start smaller.

<!-- Turn 65, round z3, a fresh writer on the middle paragraph alone (job lm1p2): if even an imagined caring grown-up makes you feel more threatened or lonelier, go smaller still, to the least cruel response a decent adult could offer; scaffolding, not a test of whether you were loved enough. Alone Human Written (0.17, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/2736e072-e9a6-45fc-b465-b89760db860d; the opening with it Human Written (0.25, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/4b86c5ce-675f-45a4-b50a-23130bddce39. -->

Maybe just imagining a caring grown-up makes you feel more threatened, or lonelier. Lonelier is a strange one, with somebody right there. Then go smaller still, to the least cruel response a decent adult could offer your little one. It's scaffolding.

<!-- Turn 65, round z, fresh writer 1, the h1's P3 with its grounding's two MUST fixes (by choice, once the guarding part has been heard; this guard is about letting the feeling in, and people still earn trust): taking the hostility out makes room for warmth; letting defenses down; the ego as a rigid security guard; a cold part standing in front of the warmth (Hear the Protective Part First). Alone Human Written (0.055, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/806531d3-1254-4296-8b22-e0e44d529be5. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 77.1 A: the cross-reference now names Hear the Guard First. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

Taking the hostility out makes room for warmth. What people most often say helped is letting their defenses down. I mean by choice, after the part guarding you has been heard. If you've never been safe being loved, your ego can act like a rigid security guard that won't let the feeling in, and being vulnerable is the opening the feeling needs. (This guard is only about letting the feeling in. People still have to earn your trust.) Sometimes the warmth is there, with a cold part standing in front of it, guarding something. That part tends to step aside once it's been heard (see the section Hear the Guard First).

<!-- Turn 101 (his answers of 2026-10-23 14:05 UTC, experiments/t101-answers/JOEL-20261023-1405.md): 91.1 B: old P6's first sentence is cut as a near-repeat of this paragraph's last; its second ("That takes warmth, and if there isn't any yet, borrow some.") ends this paragraph now. -->

<!-- Turn 102 (his answers of 2026-10-23 17:53 UTC, experiments/t102-answers/JOEL-20261023-1753.md): 94.2 A was conditional on a Pangram check. With GPT's sentence ("If you have advice, let them take it in at their own pace.") in place of his two, this paragraph read 100% AI alone (https://www.pangram.com/history/bf6562e2-577e-4fce-a12e-af98c12c7688, ledger borrow-p5#144), where his version read 100% Human (borrow-p5#134), so his two sentences stay. -->

If the love still isn't coming, you can at least stop judging your little one. Obviously no insults. Helpful advice is great, we all need advice. Let them absorb it at their pace. And don't expect a thank-you for the session, like they booked it. That's enough to start being the adult, even if it isn't enough yet for them to trust you. That takes warmth, and if there isn't any yet, borrow some.

Your little one might not take it well at first. 😜 More like "Oh, so NOW you care?" Your first instinct might be "But I'm trying!" Some days, just hearing it out like a decent adult is the only thing you'll manage to borrow.

I sometimes ask: "How would I respond to this once I understood it more clearly, in balance and with compassion?" It's a question borrowed from your future self, and I wouldn't take their answer as gospel. I don't think a perfect Higher Self is waiting in there with the answers, either (more on that in How This Relates to IFS).

You can borrow a wiser future version of whoever else is involved, too, as long as you don't assume they'll end up on your side, or sorry. What the current one keeps doing is still what you go by, however good the picture looks (the future one has never called you back).

My father often suggested folks remember a similar-level (or even harder) challenge that they were able to handle. Maybe you survived a car crash and surgery a few years ago, but now you're having trouble with emotional eating. You can draw strength from the past. As my sister, a historian, likes to say, ["The past didn't go anywhere."](https://theanarchistlibrary.org/library/april-rosenblum-the-past-didn-t-go-anywhere) Your own challenge doesn't need to be as dramatic as a car crash, as long as it really happened. So who or what helped you get through it? Is one piece of that still within reach? If you catch yourself thinking "I survived a car crash, so why can't I stop eating when I'm upset?", that's the past turned into a stick to hit yourself with. Maybe you're more worn out now, or missing whatever helped back then. (No example? Try another doorway.)

## Notice the Part That Came Here

<!-- Turn 65, round z, fresh writer 2 (LM dedup group 2): No. I hate myself, given an honest hearing and not argued with; opening this article, coming to therapy, asking for help, trying again after deciding you were hopeless: something in you wanted things to be different. Six of my P1s read AI. Alone Human Written (0.42, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/53633e96-552d-46b6-b5d6-f9fc2ff88393; the h2 Human Written (0.16, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/501f6222-db01-48c3-bc54-72bd7d3b14f5. -->

Some people hear all this about love and answer, "No. I hate myself." If that's what you find, it deserves an honest hearing. Just listen to it, without arguing back. But if you opened this article, something in you wanted things to be different, whatever the rest of you was saying. The same goes if you came to therapy or asked someone for help. Or maybe you'd already decided you were hopeless, and then for some reason you tried again.

<!-- Turn 65, round z, fresh writer 2: what that something was (hope, desperation, curiosity, fear, duty, not wanting to keep suffering, maybe love), no need to decide; ask that part what it hoped might happen when it brought you here, and follow that thread. Alone Human Written (0.23, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/576c9c1e-3442-40c8-8874-16aabdedf924. -->

What was it? It might have been hope, or desperation. Maybe you were curious, or scared. Some people come here out of duty. Or it was more like, "I'm not going to keep suffering like this." And maybe it was love. You don't need to decide which. Ask that part what it hoped might happen when it brought you here, and follow that thread.

## Borrow Love—or Borrow the Perspective of Care

<!-- 2026-10-15 (turn 56): Joel's P1, word for word, in place of mine (10:43 UTC, his answer to question 27: "27 this passes"; experiments/t56-answers/JOEL-ANSWERS-20261015-1043.md). His check counts; this h2 and the h1 with it are combinations he didn't check. -->

Sometimes it's not that you can't love in general. Your dog can chew up your good shoes and be forgiven before you've found the other one. But maybe you direct that love to your little one and the attempt goes flat, or starts to feel weirdly unsafe. Just feel that love you can feel for others for a minute, in your body, before you try to redirect anything. Then as you relax into that and enjoy it more, imagine it flowing downhill, like an overflowing river of delicious chocolate. Now imagine how happy your inner child is at the bottom of the hill to play in that chocolate love that rolled down!

Watch how it lands, though. "I care about you" can be patient, or it can be "I care about you, now hurry up and feel better." Getting close can be too much, too, the way kids squirm away from an aunt they barely know who goes in for the big hug, and then climb all over the uncle who's just sitting there. And if nothing moves at all, don't push it. Knowing that love is in you somewhere counts for a lot, and you're borrowing something you already know how to do.

<!-- 2026-10-07 (turn 31), E137 sweep: "And the gap it gets around even has a name" for "And this trick even has a name" (the paradox is the gap, wiser about others' conflicts than your own; the trick narrows it). Alone 100% Human (112). This h2 is Mostly Human, 4% AI (794), and the h1 4% AI (1,609), both from the open E117 window in Ask God for a Loan P1; the h2 as installed without this fix has the same window (791, 4% AI), so the fix doesn't move it. -->

Or put somebody you care about into your exact life, your family, your bank account and all, and then step outside it and ask what you'd tell them. It's a [cheap trick for becoming the outsider to your own mess](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21728454/). The quest for self-knowledge is a life's journey, but you don't have to wait for your life to finish to figure out your best next move. And the gap it gets around even has a name, [Solomon's paradox](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24916084/), after the king everybody brought their disputes to, who then wrecked his own life.

Then take a small piece of whatever you'd tell them and hand it to yourself. If the advice was "get more sleep," then for now just try brushing your teeth before midnight instead of at two. That's how you learn as a kid, and it works now, too. The technical term for this is "[scaffolding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instructional_scaffolding)."

### Show Up When Nothing's Wrong

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E7 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e7 a") -->

If the only time the Nurturer shows up is when something's wrong, your little one can start to feel like they're your job. Sometimes show up just to say "I'm glad you're here," and then enjoy them for a minute, the way you'd enjoy a little kid who wants to show you a rock they found. Or picture the two of you washing the dishes together, nobody asking anybody how they feel.

### A Smaller Doorway: Goodwill

Sometimes "I love you" is more than you can honestly say, especially to somebody who's treated you badly. Even the usual whittled-down loving-kindness prayer, "May you be happy," can feel like rewarding them. That's why I go with Buddha's overall wish (paraphrasing): **"May you be loving, peaceful, and free,"** since if they were all of those, they wouldn't have done what they did, and then they actually would be lovable.

This goodwill isn't just for the target of it, though. You may find it bounces back and opens up a little warmth in your heart. And if "I love you" goes flat when you aim it at yourself or your little one, the same wish works there, too. 🫶🏿

### Ask God for a Loan

The dog kind of love is real love. Some people also know a love that's a whole different size, like God's, or whatever opened up for them in prayer or on a long retreat, and I don't want to lump that in with the dog. If you've been there, don't shrink it down to a thought experiment about a friend.

If you haven’t been there yet (and even if you have), I have good news for you!

#### Your Loan is PRE-APPROVED!

<!-- Native YouTube embed (Joel, 2026-10-14): a short from EndSickness.org about how gratitude helps the nervous system (https://www.youtube.com/shorts/4TYvq-d0-XM). -->

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

One practical door *anyone* can use to slowly experience divine love is gratitude (even after the Turkey’s done & Thanksgiving is over!). Genuinely cultivated gratitude (not just empty/forced ritual) is one way to experience divine love for the first time, or to get it back when the feeling fades. I think it’s essential if we want to live in that love instead of occasionally visiting it.

<!-- Turn 78: Joel's change to this paragraph (2026-10-21 00:49 UTC, experiments/t78-answers/JOEL-20261021-0049.md), word for word: the parenthesis after "That’s something to be grateful for" is new. His paste ended at "toxic positivity.”"; his next two sentences, on grief and on the psychedelic trip, are kept until he says otherwise (question 73). Sent to the InnerSignal map as a suggested fix. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

This is a bit tricky to put into words, but let me try anyway… You may be suffering now, but you could be suffering more. That’s something to be grateful for (although please don’t fall into the common trap of being grateful that you have what others don’t have, as that’s more like selfishness). I’m not talking just about changing your “glass half empty” perception to a glass “half full perception.” I’m not pushing “toxic positivity.” Being thankful shouldn’t be pushed on people who just need to grieve for the moment. On the other hand, I’ve noticed that I can sometimes completely flip a bad psychedelic trip into a good one with gratitude alone.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 101 (his answers of 2026-10-23 14:05 UTC, experiments/t101-answers/JOEL-20261023-1405.md): 93.2, his two sentences added onto this paragraph, as he pasted them: "my bad, i was editing the version i had before that. we can add that onto the para about prosperity philosophy". -->

And I’m certainly not preaching “prosperity philosophy,” even though it can seem to start at that. We still need to remember that if we can only be thankful for the good things we have, we can fall into the trap of thinking that what we have is happiness itself. I’m ultimately reaching for something much deeper. Because getting what you want is an endless loop, and it doesn't lead to being content with less. When you can see that less is more, at a deeper level, that's where the real gratitude flourishes.

If you have a moment right now to breathe, that’s something to be grateful for. I learned this many times the hard way. For example, when I got tuberculosis from a cave in Mexico at age 20, and coughed for six months straight, eventually literally unable to breathe, leading to a near death experience. And then in Senegal, I had a suspected bilharzia infection in 2021, and could only painfully pee blood, I promised to always be thankful if I could ever pee normally again. Miraculously, I then found some obscure ethnobotanical research (on [Google Scholar](http://scholar.google.com), before AI) which led me to drink roselle tea. Within an hour, I was cured. I don’t always remember to say “thank you” when I pee now, but I try.

That may sound like a silly, quirky waste of time to some folks. But what I notice is that saying thank you for every little thing, whenever I remember to, is not about imposing some kind of “spiritual discipline.” It is directly reconnecting me to something pure and sacred.

<!-- Turn 100 (his message of 2026-10-23 13:10 UTC, experiments/t100-answers/JOEL-20261023-1310.md): his updated paragraph, word for word, with his link; it starts where the paragraph above used to go on, so the paragraph above is the old one's first half, unchanged (question 93.3). -->

And even (or especially) when I am suffering, I try to remember to be grateful for that also, because if not for suffering, I would not even think about how much I have enjoyed not suffering as much, and how much this tribulation will help me to develop more compassion, wisdom, and gratitude in future. It connects me with the plight of the countless other beings suffering at that very moment, authentically bringing up a [wellspring of compassion in my heart for all of us](http://love.u-dont-exist.com). And I see clearly that it is my inner child who is feeling this pain, and what an honor it is that I can be there for him.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 100 (his message of 2026-10-23 13:10 UTC, experiments/t100-answers/JOEL-20261023-1310.md): his updated paragraph, word for word ("i also updated 2 paragraphs in ask god for a loan you're pre-approved"). It leaves out "nor does gratitude for difficulties justify staying with an abuser" and the two sentences after "That’s something to be grateful for." (the endless loop, less is more): questions 93.1 and 93.2. -->

<!-- Turn 101 (his answers of 2026-10-23 14:05 UTC, experiments/t101-answers/JOEL-20261023-1405.md): 93.1, his words for this paragraph's first sentence: "right, change the next sentence in next para to: \"Of course, that’s not to say you should not try to suffer less, or get away from abusers\"" (the period added). -->

Of course, that’s not to say you should not try to suffer less, or get away from abusers. The point of life, according to every spiritual tradition and religion, I think, is to suffer less, which first requires us to understand the cause of suffering, and what real suffering and real happiness are. If you, like Biggie Smalls, have come to understand that having money is not real happiness, that’s a big deal! That’s something to be grateful for. If we get a chance to meditate or pray today, while on the toilet, upon waking up, while ChatGPT is “thinking” for us, that’s something to be thankful for, for sure.

Notice how, when you offer thanks (to the universe, to life, to God, whatever you may believe in), a space in your heart opens up. It might not feel immediately like you suddenly know the real meaning of Divine Love, but it’s a little spark of … something.... There’s a tiny bit of freedom, of peace, stillness, softness. And as that softness deepens each time, you start to understand what I mean by Divine Love. Many people have written books on this topic (like [A. Helwa’s book](https://authorahelwa.com/), Secrets of Divine Love, for example), so I won’t belabor the point further here, but I hope you are able to experience this when you try it out for a while, and I’d really love to know how it works for you!

##### Invite Your Little One In

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E8 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e8 a") -->

Once you start to feel that lovely softness of the heart, invite your little one in with you as your spiritual integration practice. They might go numb, or call BS on that. If so, they might just need to see if you're actually humble enough, and grateful enough for them, to listen to their rejection and be patient. If you instead retreat back into your cozy corner, it's the food bank feast all over again: You can have an enormous spiritual feast upstairs while the child is still starving downstairs.

### When the Spiritual Relationship Hurts

If a spiritual teacher you trusted betrayed you, even sitting down to meditate can feel like it belongs to them now. Kids quit piano over one bad teacher, and this is way worse. But if you let an abuser control your appreciation of life, then you've given them an unearned win.

Pangram 4.0, dashboard, try 2: alone Human (69); this h3 Human (126); the Borrow Love h2 Human Written, high (1,386). Record: experiments/t54-askgod/. -->

Being the grown-up doesn't mean controlling everything yourself, though. After a betrayal like that, part of you might want to. Spiritual support can help you act where it's yours to act, and let go of outcomes and other people's choices where it isn't. The [Serenity Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serenity_Prayer) is the example I'd give, if prayer still feels like yours.

## Become the Adult Apprentice

Say a friend sits with you while you fall apart. Most of us feel better and call them again the next time, and they get a little better at it every time while we don't. So steal some of it. Usually what helped was how they just sat there without trying to fix you. Ten seconds of sitting with yourself like that is good enough for a start, so try it before you call next time.

# Building Trust With Your Little One

## Not Every Hero Wears A Cape

<!-- Turn 107 (his answers of 2026-10-25 00:24 UTC, experiments/t107-answers/JOEL-20261025-0024.md): 103.2, his own first three paragraphs, word for word ("i fixed the section, it's all dark green now"). Make It Intentional and its two paragraphs stay as they were -->

Set one boundary. “What’s a ‘boundary’?”, I might be asking. That looks like saying no to something that’s costing you too much (money, time, whatever it is). Handle that task you’ve been putting off, or the tax man, or your DUIs in Maryland, even if all you do today is open the envelope. Keep one small promise to your little one. Clean your room! Maybe your little one had grown-ups who said sweet things and then did nothing, and maybe you’ve done it too, so this is how they start to believe you.

You can protect your little one from yourself too, like by repairing after an internal attack. “What’s an ‘internal attack’?” Calling yourself an idiot for losing your keys again, for one. Your little one was listening, so you go back and apologize to them. If they’re sarcastic about it, you hear it out instead of snapping back. They don’t trust you yet, and you don’t ask them to. They complain about something, and if they’re right, you say so. All of that is evidence they're accepted and you’re safe to be around. Love needs those to start with, but without warmth they can still feel like they’re only being put up with.

Plenty of people are still at the putting-up-with part. Even with little or no warmth for your little one yet, the relationship can start with the Protector’s plain acts and the warmth can come after, which feels backwards, because you’d think you have to feel it first before you could do anything for them. But yeah, bonding starts somewhere. And this is not to say you’ve never done anything to care for yourself. If not, you’d be dead right now. But we’re making additional acts of care intentional now as a base for love to grow.

### Make It Intentional

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E9 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e9 a") -->

Intentional, meaning you pick one of those acts and decide when you'll do it, and what the smallest first step is (like with the envelope). If it needs money or somebody's help, sort that out before the day, and if you'll have to tell someone something they won't like, say it out loud to yourself ahead of time. Then put yourself in their shoes to see how it sounds, and go back & forth like that a couple times until both sides feel good about it, or good enough, as long as what you needed to say is still in there.

After the day, look at what actually happened. Say you did the Protector action(s) and you don't feel any better, or you feel worse. Standing up for yourself can do that at first. Not doing it is harder to figure out. Fear is the first guess. But did you know how to do it, and why? Were you worn out? Maybe whoever said they'd help couldn't make it. 🤗

## Love Doesn't Have to Wait for Trust ❤️

Sometimes the love is there for your little one and still feels unsafe to them, or safe for a minute and then not. Love and trust don't have to come as a pair, but they got tied together, as if one had to vouch for the other. So your love can sound like you're asking them to trust you. It doesn't need their trust, but it does need you to be honest with them, which isn't the same as perfect.

<!-- 2026-09-30 (turn 7): P2 took three rounds of fresh writers. Round one (a, b, c) passed grounding, but the cold reads couldn't follow its evidence list, and a2 was 100% AI (73 words). The love can go first, though, before their trust is done going over your track record (they've heard what you still call yourself when you mess up, sometimes out loud). Your little one says "I don't trust you." You answer, "I believe you. And I love you."

<!-- 2026-09-30 (turn 7): P3 is the guide's bedtime story in Joel's first person, nothing added. I was about five when my mom told me it was time for bed (or something equally outrageous to a five-year-old). I cried and screamed "I HATE YOU!" 😡 She answered, "But I love you!" 🥰

<!-- 2026-09-30 (turn 7): P4 is fresh writer a with one fix. That wording came from Claude's brief, and the writers copied it. Fixed: "Fights don't usually go like that." Writer b's "She never told me" was dropped as a claim about his whole childhood. Pangram 4.0: alone 100% Human (66 words, short text); h2 + P1–P4 100% Human (220 words). 2026-10-01 (turn 8): the last sentence ("And she didn't tell me I wasn't angry.") is cut. Joel, 00:12: a double negative with an unclear "I", and "not what happened (i mean it's true she didn't, altho maybe she did, you don't know)". It came from the guide, whose "I" is an AI draft. Pangram 4.0 without it: alone 100% Human (56 words, short text); the section 100% Human (382). A proposed P4 with his own explanation of why fights don't go like that is in the in-context page, not here. -->

That completely threw me. Fights don't usually go like that. Anger is a way to get more power when you feel a lack of justice or agency. You puff yourself up so you seem frightening or dangerous, or you demand to be heard. My anger was there to get me heard, and her answer showed me I didn't need it for that. Whatever injustice I thought there was wasn't coming from a lack of love, either. So the anger seemed stupid. I saw for myself that it was only hurting me, without helping me see clearly or get what I wanted. Still, what I screamed was true right then, and what she said back was true too, so I could hate somebody who loved me and she'd still be my mom the whole time.

Cold read: every line OK. Grounding: every line OK. Alone 100% Human (69), P5 try 2; Love Doesn't Wait with it 100% Human (1,194), turn 22, try 1 (API). -->

When it's your little one yelling at you, though, try not to turn "But I love you" into a line you say on cue, because then it can brush the anger off, and what it's really saying is "so stop being angry." I think you can mean it and still be doing that. Does the love stay afterward and take whatever's true in the anger seriously?

<!-- 2026-09-30 (turn 7): P6 carries the guide's second exchange in Joel's voice, both lines exact. Fresh writer c, with one fix by Claude: "You can answer" → "You answer" (P2's form). The linter hard-failed all three drafts on coach density: one "you can" in a frame of about 24 words, once Joel's lines were excluded with --owner. Cold read: every line OK. Grounding: every line OK. Pangram 4.0 won't check it alone (47 words); h2 + P1–P6 100% Human (341 words). -->

The love can stay, and your little one might still come back with "Big fuckity whoopty doo. I don't believe you." 🙄 You answer, "Okay. I believe that you don't believe me. I still love you. 🤗 You don't have to love me back. Tell me what feels unsafe."

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 10): P7 is the turn-9 proposal, installed as proposed (Joel, 04:53 UTC: "P7 proposal is good."): fresh writer b of three with two cuts by Claude (its 😬 and "same as before"), and a fresh writer's last sentence from the grounding review's good-to-great line (why the swearing runs out). Pangram 4.0, turn 9, try 1: the proposal alone 100% Human (82 words); the section 100% Human. Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turns 9 and 10. -->

Your little one might say it's you. You don't have to accept every conclusion they reach about you, but do listen for the concrete complaint without fighting the yelling and swearing around it. If it's something serious, answer it seriously. The complaint might only be one sentence, once the swearing runs out. Like the anger at bedtime, the swearing is there to get them heard, so once you're listening instead of fighting, they don't need it anymore.

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 10): P8 carries what the film's characters say (E98), in place of turn 9's summary. Joel, 04:53 UTC: "why not just check what was actually said for improving the vagueness?" Transcript: https://scrapsfromtheloft.com/movies/beautiful-day-in-neighborhood-transcript/ ; checked against the screenplay: https://thescriptsavant.com/movies/A_Beautiful_Day_In_The_Neighborhood.pdf . Nobody in the exchange mentions Fred's work or projects. The last sentence is the turn-9 proposal's, which Joel approved ("The P8 proposal was good."). Three rounds of cold-read fixes: what he told Lloyd about his sons, "about his dad", "he and his youngest". Pangram 4.0, the paragraph alone: 100% Human on tries 1, 2 and 3 (149, 153, 153 words); the section 100% Human. -->

You can see the same move in a dramatized scene from the movie A Beautiful Day in the Neighborhood. Lloyd, a reporter, tells Mr. Rogers, "I can't imagine it was easy… growing up with you as a father." He doesn't collapse under that or fight it, but he does answer what's worth answering in it. He tells Lloyd that until recently his oldest son never told people about his dad, and his youngest "genuinely tested" him. Eventually he and his youngest found their way, and now he's very proud of both his sons. Then he says, "But you are right, Lloyd. It couldn't have been easy on them. Thank you. Thank you for that perspective." Like the two truths at bedtime, a true complaint and real love don't cancel each other out, so you can take the true part without giving up your love or defending yourself.

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 11): P9 is Joel's (13:57 UTC: "it absolutely does make sense to finish that thought otherwise it's unclear"), Human, medium confidence on his Pangram check. He took the proposal's sentence and made its unclear parts plain ("The resentment can be purely visceral" for "is the bedtime anger from the other side"; "so the accusation has nothing to win" cut), and ended on "Be careful not to take the accusation as proof your love really is conditional, when it could just be another part talking." Before it, turn 10: fresh writer b of three, rebuilt over five versions (Pangram alone: 31% AI, 100% Human, 100% Human, 35% AI, 100% Human); the why kept failing, at the ending. Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turns 10 and 11. -->

Also notice what else in you answers your little one, besides what you choose to say. When they say "I don't believe you," something in you might fire back an accusation, and then they can learn your love is only there as long as they take it the right way. The resentment can be purely visceral: being doubted after you've tried feels unfair, but there's nothing unfair in them going over your track record. The part that resents not being believed might be the same one that said "I love you" in the first place, or a different one, or a bit of both. Both talk in your grown-up voice, so to your little one they're the same. Be careful not to take the accusation as proof your love really is conditional, when it could just be another part talking.

### Instead of Blame

<!-- Turn 101 (his answers of 2026-10-23 14:05 UTC, experiments/t101-answers/JOEL-20261023-1405.md): 91.2 A: the second half of this section, from blaming an age of you to what you can give your little one now, under its own heading. -->

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 11): P10, the guide's "When life has gone badly, the internal system may try to recover control by finding a defendant…" (G10). Four cold reads stumbled on the narrated charge ("the grown-up who was supposed to save everybody": who's everybody, who decided, it reads backwards), so the charge became the blaming part's own line; grounding wasn't re-run on that rewording, which carries the same claim. Pangram 4.0, the paragraph alone: 100% Human on every try (six, all clarity fixes; this text 75 words, try 6); the section 100% Human (892). The cold read of this text: every line OK. Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turn 11. -->

Your little one might not be the only one getting accused. When life has gone badly, part of you may try to get some control back by finding someone to blame, like "If you'd gotten it together at fifteen, you could have saved everybody." If it's somebody's fault, it didn't have to happen, and that feels like control. But figuring out which age of you is to blame doesn't give you what it would have taken back then. That part may start picking on a different age.

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 12): P11 is Joel's (15:59 UTC: "for P11, this one is human and works better"), built on Claude's rebuild draft c, which was 100% AI alone. He added "And actually, not to beat a dead horse here, but" and "Hindsight being 20/20 doesn't change that.", and cut "and that includes" / "even if they seem like the one who should have known better". Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turn 12. -->

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 98.3 A (his "98. agree cut all"): cut "You still did what you did, though, and if it hurt people or damaged a life, that may need repair.". The original is in experiments/t102-safety/SAFETY-CUTS.md. -->

Whichever age it picks on, saving everybody was never your job. At fifteen you probably didn't even have your own money, or anywhere else to go. And actually, not to beat a dead horse here, but every age of you was stuck with whatever it had then, even the adult you were last year.

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 12): P12, the guide's six questions (G12), as a numbered list (the plan: "a list that carries the meaning"). Fresh writer a of three; all three were 100% Human alone on try 1 (a 82 words, b 85, c 72). Cold read and grounding on a and on b: every line OK, nothing open. Joel adopted the proposal at 17:50 UTC ("P12 proposal accepted"): the line after the list, by a fresh writer from the reviews' good-to-great line (100% Human alone, 95 words, try 1). Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turn 12. 2026-10-03 (turn 21): questions 5 and 6 are Joel's, word for word (01:06 UTC), each in place of a list of three; OWNER-EDITS ldw-p12-q5-q6. -->

So instead of asking which age of you deserves the blame, I'd ask these:

1. What was that age of you trying to get, or get away from?
2. What did it understand back then?
3. What options could it actually see? Could it stand to take any of them?
4. What happened because of the choice it made?
5. What was missing? Some ability, or help/protection from someone else?
6. What can you learn or repair now, and how would you act differently in such situations?

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 78.1, ST-CA candidate 10 (sort the guilt; section 17.1.1, PDF pp. 476 to 483), one sentence before this paragraph's line. Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

It can help to sort the guilt, too, since some of it was put on you by people who needed someone to blame, and some is only the critic (ST-CA sorts guilt into four piles, in section 17.1.1). Whatever was missing back then, you can start giving your little one now.

<!-- 2026-10-01 (turn 12): P13, the guide's "Big fuckity whoopty doo—what are you gonna do for me? is still a literal question…" (G13), with its list of evidence made a callback to Not Every Hero Wears A Cape (the plan). Fresh writer a of three; a and c were 100% Human alone on try 1 (73 and 80 words). Cold read and grounding on a: every line OK. Joel adopted the proposal at 17:50 UTC ("P13 proposal accepted"): the sentence after the second, by a fresh writer from the grounding's GREAT line (100% Human alone, 106 words, try 1). On c, the cold read couldn't place "Then … comes back" after the list, and grounding flagged "Before you've done a single thing for them" (CONTRADICTS the article's "this is not to say you've never done anything to care for yourself"). Record: experiments/LOVE-DOESNT-WAIT-20260930.md, turn 12. -->

Your little one has their own version of that last question: "Big fuckity whoopty doo. What are you gonna do for me?" They actually want to know, though I think the first answer may be that you keep loving them, even while they don't believe you. What you do for them takes days to add up, but the moment they doubt you, you can show them right away that the love stays and doesn't turn into an accusation. Then their trust can grow from the stuff back in "Not Every Hero Wears A Cape," like hearing out the sarcasm, or finally opening that envelope.

If your promises still mean something to your little one, maybe because you've kept a few small ones, a quiet vow can help too. As many real-life parents know, your heart really explodes in such an unexpected and wonderful way when you hold your newborn baby for the first time (happened to me!). And if you're not yet a parent, it can really help to imagine holding a baby in your arms to make the vow feel real. Imagine you just had a baby, how would you feel? Would you look at him/her with adoration and joy? Carry that into visualizing yourself with your own inner child:

Or even before that, just promise your little one you won't be cruel to them on purpose. Borrow the Nurturer to improve this.

Pick one thing to do that's small enough to repeat. A big gesture you later drop would only prove them right to doubt you, but a small thing you keep doing can change their mind.

Whenever your little one shows up looking for their parent, even if they don't know that's what they are doing, you go back to being the one they can rely on, and you're kind to them while you do it. If they show up to have some innocent fun, don't "put them in their place" as the adult. But sometimes they show up as the one who doesn't want to go to bed. Now you're in my mom's place: you still go to bed, and staying kind about it shows them bedtime isn't a lack of love.

### Remind Yourself

<!-- 2026-10-02 (turn 15): P1 is Joel's (00:16 UTC), word for word, with his mismatched quote marks on the note made a matching pair: his merge of the guide's G1 and G3, with "a photo of yourself as a child" ("i hear people like to keep childhood photos"). The vow can live somewhere you'll see it, too, like on a stuffed animal from when you were little, a photo of yourself as a child, or just a sticky note on the bathroom mirror. Whatever helps you feel connected to the Nurturer and Protector you've been practicing. The Teddy bear note may remind you to think, "When I see you, I remember to stop attacking myself and go to bed on time."

<!-- 2026-10-02 (turn 15): P2 is Joel's (00:16 UTC), word for word: the guide's pet paragraph (G4), with "A real-life child of yours" added ("I actually used my son ... to reconnect with my inner child, and I think that's very common for parents"). Human, low confidence on his check; with P1, Human, medium. On Claude's, 100% Human alone (51, short text), try 1. -->

A real-life child of yours, or a calm pet, can also act as a companion that brings out the relationship with your own inner child. But those are also their own beings with their own needs as well. Let them come and go without making them responsible for the session.
