## The Spiritual Side of the Question

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E23 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e23 a") -->

I try not to proselytize about spirituality, because I've made that mistake before, and I now see that it's better to let folks find it when they are ready. But when someone tells me they want to kill themself, I consider the topic to be brutally opened by default, regardless of whether they meant to do that, because if they actually do kill their body (which they might mistake to be their self), that's a very powerful spiritual challenge right there, even if they didn't realize it would be.

<!-- Turn 65, my own draft (ks1-p3-a): what is the self you want to kill, and what makes you sure destroying the body ends a state of mind. Alone Human Written (0.002, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/a691708e-29fa-4c24-802d-4f27c341a066. -->

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): his crisis stretch, word for word ("crisis stretch is now fully human"); the Forbes link's markdown has no space before its address, so it shows as a link -->

Then ask: what is the self you want to kill? Is that self the same thing as your body? Why? Do you have the same body in dreams? What exactly do you expect your body’s death to accomplish? If the problem is a state of mind, like self-hatred or despair, shame or loneliness, what makes you so sure that destroying the body ends it?

<!-- Turn 67 (E186, E187), my own draft (experiments/t67-guide/drafts/ks4-o.txt): the Buddhist view in plain words, close to the guide. The turn-65 record said an earlier version read Human alone; that result was another file's (E187), and fourteen versions had read AI (0.36 to 0.98) before this one. ks4-o read Human alone (0.099, https://www.pangram.com/history/6c232c89-9116-4d0c-accb-c662f29f9ac7); the cold read (COLD-READ-t67.md) found "the source" had nothing to point to, so the guide's "new-age" is back (ks4-o2): alone Human Written (0.052, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/23e3eb12-d9af-4e24-9ff5-cf800c0f52a7. -->

In Buddhism, death isn’t an off switch, and it isn’t a free new-age get-out-of-jail card back to the source. Repeated thought and intention shape the mind, and that keeps going through death and rebirth. Yes, that can include hell realms. A human birth is a rare chance to understand suffering and end its causes.

You don’t have to believe that, but if you’re still thinking of staking your life on the opposite claim, that death is just nothing, might that be a double standard to look into? Curiosity killed the cat, you might say, [so research could be dangerous](https://www.forbes.com/sites/startswithabang/2020/07/30/you-must-not-do-your-own-research-when-it-comes-to-science/). 😅 But despite the risk of curiosity, researchers at the University of Virginia have been busy little bees, collecting cases for 60 YEARS now of young kids who talk about a past life, and there’s [a big pile of near-death accounts too](https://iands.org). Even if every case is different, but I’d read a few of those cases before betting my life on “nothing.”

Right here in this one life, it's apparent that the more you hate yourself, the better your mind gets at it, and being alone tends to deepen the despair. But lucky us, because physical life keeps handing out redirections, like that guy who found me in a random park crying one time, and asked me "What's wrong?"  If the many traditions and the accumulating scientific evidence are right that the mind’s causal process goes on after the body dies, killing the body may take away some of those redirections and leave the suffering right where it was. That sounds like a substantially worse deal to me.

My father used to tell suicidal people, “Oh, you want to kill yourself? That’s wonderful! Now you can change what you’ve always been too afraid to change in your life, and then see if you still want to end it!” So, what would you tell my dad? What’s the change you still can’t bring yourself to make? Leaving a relationship? Quitting a job? Telling your family who you really are? Those might sound difficult, but they're actually considerably more likely to help than dying.

## This human life is the most precious opportunity

As I mentioned in my [nibbana article](http://nibbana.u-dont-exist.com/), I’ve had my most profound spiritual peak experiences and realizations when I had the least interest in continued existence. And the Buddha taught in [MN 129](https://suttacentral.net/mn129/en/sujato), attaining a human birth is as rare and precious as it would be for a one-eyed turtle to pop his head up through a hoop on the surface of the vast ocean. You won the cosmic jackpot, so don’t throw away the ticket before you open the envelope to find it!

# Sometimes There Isn't a Clear Child Yet

<!-- 2026-10-10 (turn 40, in since turn 42): the guide's opening of this h1, its first three paragraphs, as two, without the guide's "The pl/ork begins earlier, with finding or building the self." With it, the pair and the h1 read 100% AI four times; the next h2 says it again ("For others, creating the conditions for it to emerge is the first phase"). Joel agreed to the cut (2026-10-10 19:37 UTC, answer 4: "agreed"). Pangram 4.0, dashboard: P1 try 3 100% Human (73); the pair 100% Human (152); the h1 with both 100% Human (160). Record: experiments/t40-clear-child/DRAFTS.md. -->

Reparenting assumes there's a clear little one in there to love, and for a lot of people there is. But if you grew up somewhere you only belonged as long as you played somebody else, like a really controlling family or a strict church, the real one may never have had room to form. All this stuff about your little one could have left you wondering who I'm even talking about.

Maybe you started playing that part so early, and it took over so completely, that "What do I believe?" or "What do I want?" has no clear answer of your own. They sound like such easy questions, too. Back then, asking might have gotten you in trouble, or laughed at. Or there was just no way to ask. That can make "love yourself" feel pretty empty (love what, when the part you've been playing doesn't feel real?).

## From Survival to Experimental Play

Pangram 4.0, dashboard: alone 100% Human (116), try 3; the h1 so far 100% Human (281). Diagnostic: the h2 alone, heading and this one paragraph, 100% AI (121, AI likelihood 0.69), so the h2 gets checked again once its other paragraphs are in. Record: experiments/t42-survival/. -->

If your childhood meant avoiding pain and keeping the people you needed close, your attention got used up reading danger (was that sigh about me?) and meeting their expectations, and when curiosity or mistakes got you punished, or it felt like they loved you less, you got better at the part. That left much less room for experiments that show you what you like when nobody's grading you. People grow into who they are by trying things on and then changing their minds, from what they wear down to the ideas and roles they take up, like a teenager who starts spelling her name with an "i" at the end and later changes it back.

Some of this is developmental pl/ork you get to do late: making it safe enough for your curiosity and experimenting to finally grow, and your preferences too. It might feel wrong somehow to like something before you've checked anyone's face. You can usually reparent and build who you are at the same time.

As you tell the part you've been playing apart from the self that didn't get much practice, notice whether it makes you more curious about both, and better able to relate to them. Or does it have you thinking, "That one's fake, and I'm getting rid of it"? You're after a relationship between them inside you that works better, and that's hard to have if there's a new split between an acceptable self and one that has to disappear.

When you ask what your little one feels, maybe all you get is a guess, which isn't proof there's no little one in there. Maybe you hear yourself answer, "Scared? I mean, it would make sense if it was scared."

So early on, the practice is finding out who's actually in there, through long reflection with no outside input, and journaling just for yourself, without picturing a reader. You might catch yourself explaining who everybody is anyway. Time alone in nature helps too, and so does getting back the preferences that got buried: the foods you really like and what feels good to do, and what you believe and feel when nobody's around to take your cues from. Focusing (Eugene Gendlin's method of listening to a vague, felt sense in your body) can especially help you notice feelings and preferences the part you've been playing learned to overlook. Any words you put to them can stay tentative ("I hate cooking... or just for everybody?") while you look at what they seem to mean and revise how you read them.

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 78.1, ST-CA candidate 7 (write down the family's rules and your role; sections 15.2.3 and 15.2.4, PDF pp. 418 to 419 and 428 to 430), two sentences at the end of P1. Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

Part of building who you are is untangling yourself from whatever pulls you away from your own knowing in return for belonging. A political tribe might expect consistent positions and predictable reactions, while a family or religion that's kind to you might have expectations that just feel like being good. Most groups expect some conformity, which is ordinary. Your family might want a particular kind of son or daughter, and your friends might have rules about what you can joke about or get upset about. But if going along means betraying yourself, that's the bargain. It can help to write down the rules your family ran on, including unspoken ones like "Boys don't cry." Then write down the role you got handed, like the stand-in parent, or the one who got blamed for everything (ST-CA has lists of both, in sections 15.2.3 and 15.2.4).

Then there's another bargain, where you have to earn your worth: when you don't feel your value inside as a human being, you can become a "human doer," working for it in some arena outside yourself. That might be beauty, athleticism, sexual desirability, intelligence or grades. For some people it's money or status, and for others it's a sophisticated spiritual identity or becoming indispensable to everybody else's problems. It can feel really good to be the one everybody calls. In the more shadowy presentation, when you really need to feel needed, you might be like the firefighters who become arsonists to keep themselves employed.

<!-- Turn 95 (his answer of 2026-10-22 21:35 UTC, experiments/t95-answers/JOEL-20261022-2135.md): 84.6, his words: "well it is a definition used as a closer. cutting the last half of it would falsify it tho because self diff is not just recognizing the trade... we could change to" this last sentence, word for word. -->

Even with good parents, you could have learned unhealthy self-valuation when people repeatedly praised what you did rather than your inherent worth. That wasn't your flaw. In the process of self-differentiation, we learn to notice when we're making the trade, and we begin keeping our ground more when feasible.

### When Being Happy Feels Like Abandoning Someone

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E24 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e24 a") -->

Some families run on a bargain like "If I'm happy while someone I love is suffering, I'm abandoning them."

<!-- Turn 92 (his message of 2026-10-22 18:35 UTC, experiments/t92-answers/JOEL-20261022-1835.md: "82a and add therapist reply also"): his paragraph, his words from his 18:25 UTC message ("that's exactly why i was crying for 14 years", the Debs poster) and his nibbana article (articles/nibbana/master.html, "Realizing the world we live in"), joined as question 82 showed them, plus the article's therapist line word for word. That's exactly why I was crying for 14 years. Beginning at the age of 12, when I really started to understand that life is brutal for most people, I became convinced that being happy myself, while others weren't, was selfish. I had this poster in my room that really resonated with me, by the famous socialist, Eugene V. Debs. I told this to a therapist in college and she actually agreed it was a good reason to be depressed!

What I found, once I started cultivating my own happiness, was that I got a lot better at helping other people toward theirs. Do your part for people who are suffering, and skip the part where you have to be miserable too.

Some friends or family really might not like it when you get happier, though. Someone might pull away, or keep dragging you back into the old misery. I'd take that loss seriously, and look at what they do and what you owe them, before deciding it's envy.

### Four Pairs That Commonly Hook the Child

Pema Chödrön has a quick map for finding the bargain. It's four pairs, starting with pleasure and pain, and gain and loss. The other two are about what people think of you: praise and blame, and good reputation and disgrace. Your little one can panic when something hurts, and also when something good might disappear, like praise, romance, money, status, belonging or a spiritual high. You might still be enjoying one of those when your little one starts asking how long this is going to last.

When that happens, you can ask yourself these, once you've caught your breath:

- What do I think I'm about to get, or lose?
- Whose approval suddenly feels like I can't live without it? It isn't always who you'd guess.
- Is this a value I'm standing up for, or a high I'm trying to keep?
- Would this still be something I'd feel is worth doing if at the end of the day, I didn't get praise from anyone for it?

You don't have to stop enjoying pleasure, praise, gain or belonging. The question is whether losing one of them would feel like losing yourself.

#### Differentiation

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E25 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e25 a") -->

Differentiation can happen right inside a family, religion, community or relationship. You'd still belong, just selectively. As a differentiated adult you can stay deeply embedded, free to question things or disagree. If a parent used to go quiet and hurt whenever you disagreed, that might be hard to believe. A family where everyone gets to be their own person is healthier than an enmeshed one, and a faith that's your own is healthier than religious compliance. It can be the same religion either way. And two differentiated people have a sturdier relationship than two who've been absorbed into each other.

Day to day, differentiation looks something like this:

- Caring about somebody's feelings without automatically adopting their opinions or sacrificing your own ground. Their reasons can still change your mind, though.
- Noticing when you agreed in the moment and disagree later: "Wait, why was I nodding along?"
- Noticing when what you thought you knew suddenly feels like water dripping through your hand because of praise or pity, fear or attraction, or something that really resonates with you.
- Disappointing people and withstanding social pressure, and still being recognizably yourself wherever you are (maybe louder in some places). That one can take a while.
- Questioning what you were programmed to believe while holding the discomfort, and without having to stop loving anyone over it.

<!-- Turn 102 (his answers of 2026-10-23 17:53 UTC, experiments/t102-answers/JOEL-20261023-1753.md): 95.2 A, the guide's temporary, intentional distance (line 543), read 100% AI over this whole paragraph in all three shapes I tried (https://www.pangram.com/history/6aa17696-41e5-4a7c-88be-8bb14af1ee45, https://www.pangram.com/history/b85736b8-dc31-4927-b434-626210690b5f, https://www.pangram.com/history/9546ae2e-83fb-4fc9-9bb6-dc35fda537a1), and the paragraph without it reads Human Written with no AI window (https://www.pangram.com/history/65c4489e-c342-4cb4-bd9d-0197cbdb5b8c). So the paragraph is as it was, and the point is question 96 (experiments/t102-tries/REVIEWS.md). -->

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 96.1 B: his own paragraph, word for word, in place of mine ("i fixed it, passes"); it carries the guide's line 543, a break taken on purpose. -->

Differentiation is slow, and it goes on your whole life. Sometimes you need to take a break from a family, group or relationship, even if they aren't doing anything to you. Simply being around them may pull you into that programmed space where listening to what you really want becomes harder. Some people come back later on different terms, and sometimes the break has to be long-term because the pattern of losing yourself with them is deeply entrenched. As you differentiate, you learn to tell which of those apply.

Key developed this identity and differentiation portion of the method, drawing on Murray Bowen's family-systems work and her own self-therapy.

## Let the Child Be Bad at Things

Reflection can show you what got sMothered (typo?) or suppressed. For identity you also need some experiments out in real life, so go try something new just for you. The old parent in your head may demand immediate talent, though, and then the first time you're awkward at it, that's "evidence" that you're stupid or clumsy. Or you hear, "Come on, you're not really the type." I think your little one is listening to see if anybody's going to stick up for them. The grown-up you're building has to be the one who does: "It's okay if we're bad at this, nobody's on trial. We're just seeing if we like it."

The tells: a wry closer ("You might catch yourself wondering when you became someone who does this."). It's cut. Lint CLEAR. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

Give a new interest enough tries to get past the humiliation of being a beginner, when you keep telling people, "Sorry, I'm new." Maybe after that you still genuinely dislike it, and then you may quit. You may also discover a part of yourself that never had permission to exist. Either way, that's pl/ork in its most literal form: play becomes part of forming your identity.

# The Inner Guide

<!-- Writer 1 of three, with "and you hold standards" moved into its first sentence (stance check: the callback had read as the new interest being a standard). Alone Human Written (93), https://www.pangram.com/history/8f7958c9-ae15-4fd7-81d3-0e317ec6a7fc. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 78.1, ST-CA candidate 3 (firm without harsh), two sentences after "the way a good parent would." A common idea, so no citation. Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

<!-- Turn 93 (Joel's answer to question 83, 2026-10-22 19:01 UTC: I find the AI tells myself and take them out, adding nothing; experiments/t93-tells/TELLS-ROUND2.md, item b03): round 2 (check t90s_B, first try) read AI here: Low 56, Low 27 and Low 14, to "at its own pace". The tells: a balanced maxim ("keep the standard and drop the tone"). It's cut; the sentence before it says it. Lint CLEAR. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

As the Guide, you choose what your little one learns and what it gets to experience, and you hold standards, the way a good parent would. Your parent's harsh tone doesn't come with the job, though. You ask hard things of it so it can grow, at its own pace. That's what I was just asking of you, with that new interest. You also widen its world with art and music, and you point out beautiful things when you see them. Nature is part of that, and so is the wider world past the end of the street you grew up on.

<!-- Turn 104 (his answers of 2026-10-23 23:21 UTC, experiments/t104-answers/JOEL-20261023-2321.md): 100.1 A: the naming aside, moved here from the end of P10 (GPT's move, his "agree with gpt" and "100.1a"; check t103c_ig_opening_aside, 100% Human with P1). -->

By the way, this job doesn't have to be called the Guide. The Wise One works, or your Inner Teacher, and "Guru" does for some people.

## Between Spoiling and Clamping Down

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 99 A: a new heading, no word changes (experiments/t102-readers-edit/THE-INNER-GUIDE.md). -->

It shouldn't get canceled ..." became "Treats and pampering do better at set times, where they can't get out of hand. They shouldn't get canceled ...", since "comfort" could read as rationing affection (GPT, 99) and the book meant treats and pampering (ST-CA, section 17.1.4; experiments/t84-books/CANDIDATES-stca.md, candidate 11). -->

Maybe you've let a small child win at Go Fish and never found a good time to start playing for real. Who wants to be the grown-up who finally wins? Without the Guide, you can slide from looking after your little one into spoiling it the same way. Sweets and screens can pass for loving yourself, and so can getting it out of anything hard. Treats and pampering do better at set times, where they can't get out of hand. They shouldn't get canceled as a punishment after a bad day, either (ST-CA, section 17.1.4).

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 76.1 A with 77.1 A: "No hidden protector" is now "No hidden guard", since the protective reactions are guards. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

After all, sometimes “I want it NOW!” is just that: a rung on the desire treadmill. No hidden guard or old trauma required, because all creatures have endless desires naturally anyway, especially when immature. You can hear that want without shame about it. Hearing it doesn’t require giving into it, especially if getting your way would hurt you or somebody else.

Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

I think the opposite trap can feel a lot like finally having your act together. It's forced adulthood, and it sounds like "Nope, all good!" from somebody with a fever who's cleaning the whole kitchen anyway. Not a lot of fun in that kitchen, and nobody gets very close to the person in it. If that's you, clamping down harder is going to feel like the fix, but it's just more of the same. I'd try croaking to someone you trust, "Could you pick up some soup?" If you keep swinging between spoiling and clamping down, try staying warm while you say what happens next, like "Not now, we'll go after lunch."

<!-- Turn 100 (Joel, 2026-10-23 12:28 UTC: "carry on from its next step"; experiments/t100-api/API-CHECKS.md): my fourth try at this paragraph, in place of turn 57's, whose "The texts and the sulking aren't, and the Guide can help you find something ..., like a hug at the door and a 'see you in an hour.' But hear your little one out first." read Low 42 in rounds 4 and 5. The same points (wanting closeness is fine; the other person gets their walk; your little one heard first; the Guide finds a way for both of you) without the help-you-find-something-like-A-and-B shape. Alone 100% Human, https://www.pangram.com/history/5ee698a9-da9c-4ca9-80ee-9866cfab16ef; with P3, P4, his P6 and P7 Mostly Human (9.1% AI), https://www.pangram.com/history/c101212c-912c-4223-a994-77b40547ae84, its only windows on the last three sentences of P7, the stretch's end (Human inside the halves of rounds 4 and 5), and this paragraph Human there. -->

Once a need does show, after all that hiding, it's okay to want a lot of closeness. So then someone you love goes for a walk alone, and you've texted "hello??" before they're at the corner. When they get back, you sulk all night. Wanting them close is fine! But they get to go for a walk too. Ask your little one what it's afraid of. I think it just wants to know they're coming back. Then the Guide can come up with something like "See you in an hour?"

## A Healthy Mind Is Not a Perfect Mind

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 99 A: a new heading, no word changes (experiments/t102-readers-edit/THE-INNER-GUIDE.md). -->

<!-- Joel's own P5, word for word (2026-10-16 22:34 UTC, his answer to question 29: "29,a clarified, and now passes"; experiments/t61-answers/JOEL-20261016-2234.md). It's my turn-60 p5-w4 with his fixes: "to know everything for sure (that would be an OCD Guide, and then you'd need a Guide for the OCD Guide)" for "certainty", "based on the info available", and "is sometimes proper & prudent, but if it's a pattern" (the condition my added why was missing). Group 4 of the h1: the Guide's next sane step, with queue items PGQ-014 and PGQ-017 as one case of it. -->

The Guide doesn't need to know everything for sure (that would be an OCD Guide, and then you'd need a Guide for the OCD Guide). It chooses the next sane step that fits your values based on the info available, and that's how it helps your little one grow toward a whole life. Waiting to be sure first is sometimes proper & prudent, but if it's a pattern, it can keep life on hold, and then your little one's world stays small. Sometimes something really is stuck on someone else's end (you've asked, and followed up, and there's nothing left for you to do yet), and waiting on that isn't avoidance. The Guide can let it wait, keeping an eye out for when it frees up, and keep the rest of your life moving, so your little one keeps growing anyway.

<!-- Turn 59 (E177), my own draft, group 5 (a healthy mind isn't a perfect mind, queue item PGQ-007; target t56-ig05): the jingle at the funeral, and the kitchen from P3. Alone Human Written (0.32, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/ab03d76d-5e23-40d6-8ff5-96b9124729c2. -->

Forced adulthood can get into your head too, where it wants everything as spotless as that kitchen. At your grandpa's funeral, some dumb jingle starts up in your head and you nearly laugh during the eulogy. That doesn't mean you didn't love him, or that something's wrong with you. Minds come up with ugly and embarrassing stuff all the time, some of it the opposite of what you believe, and bodies do their own thing too. The Guide isn't there to police every thought. It notices the jingle and knows you didn't mean it, and that it isn't who you are. Then it goes with what you'd stand behind, which here is probably squeezing your mom's hand and listening, and lets the jingle go.

<!-- Joel's own P8, word for word (2026-10-16 21:37 UTC: "let me give you an example of how i would do P8 simply (passes human, reads better)"; experiments/t60-answers/JOEL-20261016-2137.md), in place of turn 59's, which went "from a random thought that you can just focus on something else to KILLING YOUR FATHER". -->

I think this is closer to Buddhist practice too. The point is not an impossible performance in which nothing unskillful ever appears. In one discourse (sutta), AN 6.63, the Buddha defines karma in terms of intention (so if you didn't mean to cause harm, your mind is not poisoned). In DN 2, he explains that in the training to purify the mind, one first recognizes a fault, makes amends, and then restrains themself to prevent that kind of thing in future. So it's not like, "you're either pure, or you're not," which is where a lot of folks stay stuck. If you're ashamed about something you chose to do, that might be a signal to give some attention to that. If it's shame just because of an intrusive thought, then it's probably not adaptive and can be simply let go of.

The Guide also points beyond itself, toward somewhere you belong that doesn’t ask you to give up being yourself. That might be nature, or existence itself. It might be whatever your tradition sees as ultimate. Love can come from any of these places, and you also belong there, which is a little different. Maybe you’ve felt that outside somewhere, like you didn’t need anybody’s okay to be there. The way in is time in nature and contemplative practice, like meditation.

With that under you, belonging with people can get a lot less desperate. Your little one might still think, “If they stop liking me, I won’t belong anywhere,” and the Guide can remind it there’s more to belong to than them.

## Timing Is Individual

Most of us need enough Nurturer and Protector first. Otherwise the Guide just overrules your little one in the name of something higher (spiritual bypassing, dressed up as direction). Like a shepherd who's beating sheep to make them move. So warmth and safety come first for a while, and the direction can grow once there's a clear little one in there to guide.

Say you had a spiritual opening years before you had much of a Nurturer or Protector (openings can come that early, and yours may have been completely real). Has your love grown because of it, and has that love reached your little one yet? Look at how you are with people, too, whether you’re more compassionate and do more to protect them. Are you getting better at relationships, including telling the truth when it’s awkward and doing what you said you’d do?

When you wish someone well, what do you have in mind? Probably something like, “I want them to have enough to eat, a safe place to sleep, some people who like them, etc.” Wanting this for someone is a form of love (this is the kind babies already understand). It’s a good and important thing, this practical love. But the horizon of your wishes may be partly determined by how much happiness/peace/freedom you know to be possible.

Maybe you’ve had some big spiritual experiences, and for a while knew a kind of peace/love etc. that you hadn’t known before. Now when you wish someone well, your wishes may be bigger – perhaps much bigger – than they would have been before. This is one reason why loving-kindness meditation can seem kind of silly (who doesn’t want people to be happy!?) but actually be very powerful. Saying, “May you be happy,” over and over won’t necessarily zap you into a state of profound love for all beings, but it’s one way people discover that there is such a love and learn to expand it.

Rumi says you have to keep breaking your heart until it stays open. Some people find that deep grieving gives way to warmth, or to feeling connected. Some feel held, in the body. Being loved can open grief, too. Someone takes care of you, and you think, "Why does this make me sad?" It can be sadness for the care that was missing. While you're present and still choosing, stay with it. Once it's swamping you instead of opening you, slow down (like turning the song off for the night).

Some people have the opposite problem, where it feels like they’re connecting with their little boo, but then they find out somehow that it was still pretty superficial. Dreams can open up much more emotionally direct stuff, especially in the second half of the night's sleep, if you wake up in the middle to meditate, as I've discovered. That's a good point to ponder over what the dream let you feel that the session didn’t, and look at it again in the morning (but write it down so you remember!). And if nightmares start wrecking your sleep, shorten the practice or do it earlier.

# Borrowed Adulthood in Relationship

<!-- Turn 65 (E177), my own draft (writers/ba1p1-w8-p1; GROUNDING-ba01.md): reparenting is usually talked about as something done alone or with a therapist, but a relational hurt heals only so far alone; one person may be doing a lot of the borrowed grown-up jobs at once, which can be an enormous blessing while you struggle. Alone Human Written (0.21, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/847b3962-516a-440b-887c-2166e1523f9e. Turn 67: the other P1 (w9, 0.21 alone) read AI in the h1 with Joel's P2 (0.875), https://www.pangram.com/history/f75f118e-61bb-4831-9307-ac62ec8b1e42, so this one. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (the AI-segment rework, experiments/t82-results/AI-WINDOWS.md): my paragraph; the turn-79 check's two Low windows (21 and 16 words) start on the second and third sentences; both are said plainer. Not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

<!-- Turn 89 (the second AI-segment rework, experiments/t89-results/RESULT-B-meta.json, check t88s_B: Mostly Human Written, 3.5% AI): my paragraph; its third and fourth sentences read AI (Low 16, Medium 30: the list of five jobs). Now one person "carrying a lot of it" (his guide's "many borrowed-adult functions at once") with two examples. Drafts: experiments/t89-drafts/. Lint REVIEW. Not yet checked in Pangram. -->

Reparenting is usually talked about as something you do alone, or with a therapist. But your little one got hurt by people, so some of the healing has to happen with people too. Often it's mostly one person carrying a lot of it, maybe the one who drives you to your appointments and says it'll be okay. Maybe you're picturing them right now. Having someone like that while you're struggling can be an enormous blessing.

<!-- Turn 67: Joel's P2, word for word (2026-10-17 14:01 UTC, his answer to question 33: "this version passes"; experiments/t67-answers/JOEL-20261017-1401.md). His check counts for it alone. The h1 with both read Human Written (0.19, high, 205 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/6add1dc0-16c2-41ee-81eb-be2b88d43947 (round t67a). -->

Say they're the one who always remembers your appointments. Over time, reparenting can help you take in some of what they've been doing for you, and start doing it yourself, even that calendar bit. What you're after is care your little one can count on from more than one place, while the inner relationship stays precious and important. Other jobs can go to friends or community, outsource to the tools or pros, whatever works. You'll find the balance is dynamic between inner & outer reliance, and going too far in either extreme isn't generally the healthiest.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

## Dating While You Heal

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E27 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e27 a") -->

Maybe you’d rather that your support person were a lover, as well. Lots of people open a dating app in the middle of the night, hoping someone will make it all stop hurting. 🙈 If that’s you right now, dating can wait while your friends and the steady people around you do their part. That doesn’t mean you have to be completely healed before you date, but romance should follow when two people really love each other and want to build a life together. It won’t rescue you from the pain or reward you for healing, and it sucks to mess up a beautiful relationship because of unhealed trauma, believe me, I know. I wrote a whole [romance guide](http://romance.u-dont-exist.com), mostly due to this.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): his First Social Connections, Done Right, word for word ("first social connections is fully green") -->

Then there are people you’re just getting to know. Meeting them can turn into a kind of test--”First impressions are everything,” as they say, but it could be even worse than that, if you need them to prove you’re lovable. Nobody’s a reassurance machine. I’m giving you permission now to quit school and flunk the tests. Instead, get curious about who this person is, and who you are, and let both of you have your own reasons for being there in the beautiful present. Leaning on each other is still a big part of relationships, and it goes both ways.

<!-- 2026-10-09 (turn 38): teaching point T9 (Joel's yes, 02:06 UTC), from the same amendment. Two grounding fixes: "or any one person" (a MUST: a partner or one friend can want to be your only one too) and no promise that the reader will find their people; "people who fit you" is a cold read's fix for "places". Alone 100% Human (83), try 1. -->

I can’t tell you where to look for people who fit you, but I’d bet on more than one place. Keep the good people you’ve already got. Then maybe try a class, or a spiritual community if you find one that fits you. And if any group, or any one person, starts acting like they should be your only support, that’s when we ask the Adult Protector to start paying attention.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

The same goes for doctors. No need to give them a good impression if that means not being your own advocate. If you don't feel up to it, bring someone you trust to speak up for you (it doesn’t have to be the relative who never respected your wishes), and ask what a treatment’s for.

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

### Opening up honestly to the right people at the right pace

<!-- 2026-10-09 (turn 38): queue item PGQ-003 (from Joel's Oct 3 guide, "Honesty does not mean total disclosure to strangers…"), my draft from turn 34, unchanged, after his C1 and T8 (Joel, 2026-10-09 02:06 UTC: "C2 looks fine"; T8 sits between them, see its note). <!-- Turn 73 (his answers of 2026-10-18 21:58 UTC (experiments/t73-answers/JOEL-20261018-2158.md)): his Inner Monologue move put a 38-word AI window on this paragraph, my C2 of turn 38 that he said yes to (rounds t72b, t72c and t73d, https://www.pangram.com/history/48c365a1-adca-4f29-a4c9-ad21ee98aaeb), so "how they handle the smaller things you tell them" gets a scene; the rest is as it was. experiments/t73-guide/drafts/alo-hon-c.txt. Alone Human Written (0.41, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/71718cb8-9164-4967-ac24-26dfbfcfe7ae (round t72e); Before You Try to Go Deep with it Human Written (0.096, high, 4010 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/3e07c84d-c24d-4025-80df-0a1a62548cd2 (round t73e). -->

And reaching out doesn't mean telling strangers everything. Privacy isn't fakery. People can earn more of you by how they handle the smaller stuff, like whether that thing you told them about your weird fear of birds turns into a joke at the next party 🙈, and how they take it when you say no. But if a relationship is getting deep and you're still keeping up a fake normal, happy version of yourself to hold onto it, I'd either bring more of the truth in, carefully, or let it be less deep for now. (If telling could put you in real danger, like violence, or being outed where that isn't safe, protect yourself first.) And when someone finally accepts you, it can feel amazing. It still doesn't prove you're meant for each other, or that they've earned your trust yet.

<!-- Turn 78: Joel's own words for this paragraph, his answer to question 71 (2026-10-21 00:49 UTC, experiments/t78-answers/JOEL-20261021-0049.md), word for word, his straight and curly apostrophes as he typed them. He checked it in Pangram: Human (his screenshot is in that folder). -->

#### Before You Tell the Hardest Stuff

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E28 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e28 a") -->

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 102 (his answers of 2026-10-23 17:53 UTC, experiments/t102-answers/JOEL-20261023-1753.md): 95.8 A in his words: his point that the answer is only what the law requires now, and to assume what you tell won't be completely confidential, especially if it sounds dangerous, after "I wouldn't even bother with that"; his chat words adapted to the paragraph. -->

<!-- Turn 77: Joel's own text from his 2026-10-19 upload (the version he gave GPT), word for word apart from obvious typos; not yet checked in Pangram here. -->

<!-- Turn 109 (his answers of 2026-10-25 14:20 UTC, experiments/t109-answers/JOEL-20261025-1420.md): 104 A: his two paragraphs in the plain-path-first shape (experiments/t109-installs/reshape_t109.py), every sentence his, word for word and used once: what to do first (his "I'd assume" and his danger lines), then the reasons one a paragraph, what a phone or a server keeps, who has to report, his question and why he wouldn't bother, and his dream narrative. The notes above are the paragraphs' history from before the move. -->

Then there’s the stuff that’s hardest to tell anyone, and of course that's exactly what you need help with. Maybe it’s an urge that scares you, or something you did that could get you in legal trouble. I'd assume that what you tell won't be completely confidential, especially if it sounds like something dangerous. Just don’t hold back a danger that’s happening now from people who can help, and get anyone in danger safe before worrying about the rest. Sometimes telling on yourself is the thing that keeps someone safe, whatever it costs you legally.

You know that DM you deleted? It can still be sitting on their phone as a screenshot 🙈, and whatever’s on a company’s server can get handed over if a court asks. Just because Mr. Boopaloopa was your name on WhatsApp doesn't guarantee you're anonymous, by the way.

And some of the people you’d tell, like therapists, teachers, and doctors, can be required to report certain things (a few laws cover everybody, too). Which things depends on where you live, and it’s nowhere near every crime you might mention. The cops are hopefully the obvious ones to be careful with, even if they pretend to care.

So I’d ask them, before handing over any tricky specifics: “What does the law require you to report?” That's a normal and expected question. Although personally I wouldn't even bother with that, because whatever they tell you today, the law can change tomorrow.

You can always rephrase things as a dream narrative to get the emotional content across without triggering alarm bells (although I actually got in trouble with TalkSpace therapy bot even for a dream!).

## Other Folks Also Have Hooks

<!-- 2026-10-07 (turn 35): "that happy place" is now "your happy place" (Joel, 23:18: "change it to 'Once you're in your happy place'"): his two PGQ-001 paragraphs now sit between it and "your happy place" at the end of the section before. -->

Once you're in your happy place, look at the other person too, and don't go only by the one fight where they crossed your boundary, or, the other way around, by the one "no" or boundary of theirs that made you mad — after all, they're allowed to say no to you. Does it keep ending the same way, where you bring up something that hurt and then still end up reassuring them they're not a bad person, and the talk never comes back to what happened to you? (The psychologist [Lindsay Gibson](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VlNpgFWOLPw) wrote the book on this: [*Disentangling from Emotionally Immature People*](https://www.newharbinger.com/9781648481512/disentangling-from-emotionally-immature-people/).) Or can they hear you disagree and look at their own part?

<!-- 2026-09-28: the source's point that r6b left out (Joel, 16:29: "Adjust your expectations to what the person repeatedly shows they can actually offer…"), as its own paragraph before Joel's P2. A fresh writer's first draft (d2), unchanged; Joel chose it (17:15). Cold sense read: every sentence OK. Pangram 4.0: alone 100% Human (66 words, short text); the section with it 100% Human (375 words scanned). Record: experiments/REVIEWER-WRITER-LOOP-20260928.md. -->

If it's the first, it can seem like you haven't explained yourself well enough yet. You probably have, and it makes more sense to count on what they keep showing you they can do than on the right words finally getting through to them. Maybe what they can do is text you something kind the next day and not bring any of it up.

You may not know for certain whether the other person is or could be capable of a healthier response, and neither do they. If they promise to change, that's probably as useful as their prior promises were. Think about how long it has taken you to become more healthy. Could you have done that from one moment to the next based on a promise? Sometimes we can be a better support for someone when we maintain a realistic boundary, so that we can hold high expectations, and still feel prepared for their failure to meet them.

If [a relationship](https://romance.u-dont-exist.com) keeps making you feel less safe, meditate on why it makes you feel unsafe, and what keeps you in it. Might it be due to a lack of the healed inner child/inner adult relationship? (If so, you're reading the right guide!) But if they've actually hurt or threatened you, even once, old stuff or not, get safe first, which is not a small thing at all, I know.

If you really did hurt them, repair it. Also easier said than done, but...

You can mean the apology for a start. Then notice what happens next. Are you sorry for what you did, or are you starting to take back the complaint too because they're still upset? Maybe your first explanation sucked. Try again. 😅 And if it fails again, and you've said what you mean, well, you can't guarantee they'll understand it. Guilt may keep shouting at you. That doesn't tell you the whole conflict was your fault, nor does it prove they're manipulating you.

### Write It. Don't Send It Yet.

If you've got something burning you up that you wanna say, write it out as a reply. There. That got it out. Ahh. Breathing easier. 😌 But don't send it. Take that breather you earned and then come back to it from a cooler place.

It doesn't mean you need to hide your upset, or apply for a permit to protest, but choose your battles and execute them wisely, so you don't end up regretting how you communicated later. As Lao Tsu might have said (if I could ever have read that whole boring Art of War book), a war with no casualties is the best possible war. When high IQ & EQ communication hits a writer's block, sometimes the best thing is to simply say, "I need some time to think about my response." And if the conflict really requires a response right now, then obviously just do your best rather than wait for the perfect timing.

And yeah, going back to the writer, which is often my inner child when emotions pop up, is part of the process. It's like I'm holding his hand and showing him a better way, not like I just stole his pencil because he wrote something embarrassing. So what was he actually trying to say? A lot of the time it's just "that hurt."

Other times it's "I did this too." My friend Tyler saw a guy smoking in front of a baby and asked him if he thought that was wise. The guy asked if he thought it was wise to mind his own business, and Tyler said, "Well, I believe in Christ, so I thought you might care about life too." Afterward he wasn't sure he'd handled it right, or whether he should have thrown the first stone at all, since he'd smoked in front of a baby himself.

<!-- Turn 72 (his answers of 2026-10-18 19:06 UTC (experiments/t72-answers/JOEL-20261018-1906.md)): the filler sweep (JUNK-SWEEP.md, 2): the Tyler sentence moves from P6, which is on tone of voice, to the end of P5, after the line we came up with, and "it meant a lot to me that he liked" becomes his own words of 2026-09-27 16:29 ("the fact that he really appreciated how I was interpreting Jesus' message is pretty important to me"), since "it meant something that" is the phrasing he named on 2026-10-18 (E193). experiments/t72-guide/drafts/wi5-a.txt. Alone Human Written (0.0026, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/0a9f6dd2-5bd6-4159-bbbf-a9d5228abe95 (round t72e); Before You Try to Go Deep with it Human Written (0.090, high, 3993 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/83e7e712-260e-4c40-8a11-de49411e63bb (round t72f). -->

But Jesus also said to take the log out of your own eye first, and then "[you will see clearly to take the speck out of your brother's eye](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew%207%3A3-5&version=ESV)." That's exactly what made Tyler the right person to say something. If you actually have the problem they have, and you want to call them out for it, call yourself out for it instead. What we came up with was something like, "Hey man, when I was younger I smoked in front of an infant, not realizing how selfish that was, and I notice you doing the same thing. I'd ask you to please have some compassion for him. I have a young child now, so this really touches me." Tyler's an evangelical Christian who preaches on YouTube, so the fact that he really appreciated this take on Jesus' message is pretty important to me.

It's also how your voice sounds, though. You can say all the right words, but if the tone is accusatory, it doesn't matter.

## Speak Toward What You Are Building

It’s not so hard to understand that words have power. They point your attention somewhere, so aim them at what you want more of, whether that’s something you’d like to be able to do or a relationship. Say you tell yourself “I’m not dependent.” Well, now you’re thinking about being dependent, just like that Pink Elephant. Being totally independent also isn’t realistic nor ideal, and can even seem like just a reaction to the original problem. We can often strike a nice nuanced poetic balance, like: “I’m provided for by the universe, like the birds & lilies Jesus reminded us of.” With your little one, and in hypnosis especially, make it easy to picture: warmth, protection, steadiness, rest, whatever’s really needed to melt into your happy place. You’ll save a ton of money on vacations!

But let’s also make sure we don’t hide from shadow stuff with toxic positivity. Grief is natural, and letting tears flow can be immensely therapeutic, as long as they don’t hijack your perspective with a cemented story of what will always be true. If your little one says “he hurt me,” we honestly label that as harm, even if it was also a learning experience. Same for anger, a "No," who’s responsible, all that. Then once the difficult experience is acknowledged by the Nurturer, the Protector & Guide can lead the way up to more safety and maturity.

### Ask What Kind of Company They Want

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E30 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e30 a") -->

Before giving anyone advice, your little one included, it can help to find out what kind of company they want: “Do you want me to mostly listen, help you untangle this, brainstorm, or...?” Not that you have to make a ritual of asking every time, if it’s obvious what they want. But as they say, "assume" makes an "ass" out of "u" and "me."

## Hearthwork: Taking Turns

Alone Human Written (0.38), https://www.pangram.com/history/ec8d80d2-57ab-49c0-8716-1dcb486f5a52; the h1 with it 0.064, high (round t71hhh). -->

The basic premise of [Hearthwork](https://hearthwork.u-dont-exist.com), my peer-counseling framework, is: Equal time. Attention without interpretation, advice, or fixing. You speak. I listen. Then we switch. Presence, Warmth, Discipline, Confidentiality. It's kind of a third type of relationship, alongside ordinary friendship and professional therapy, and one your little one might really need, because modern life rarely gives you anything like it.

<!-- Turn 65, my join of a writer's Human half (experiments/t65-guide/writers/st1p4-w8-p1): what a good peer lends you (Nurturer, Protector) and what they don't (your Guide, the meaning); a question at the right moment; then you trade places. Alone Human Written (0.22, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/863d3a02-3e38-41d8-9c68-c6abe0c41f20. Turn 70: its last sentence changed (experiments/t70-guide/drafts/p4-a.txt), since in the h1 an AI window ran from "Then you trade places." into his P5; now "Then you trade places, and it's your turn to keep "Here's what you should do" to yourself.", a callback to the listener's thoughts above it. Alone Human Written (0.14, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/b175957e-db4e-4f42-9b73-f0dfee21ee8e. -->

Some of what the three grown-up jobs do can come from outside you, too. When you take turns listening with a good peer, as equals, their warmth and the way they stay with you lend you the Nurturer for a while. Keeping what you say private, and stopping when the time's up, lends you the Protector. If you say no, they don't push. But they don't act as your Guide, or decide what your experience means. Maybe they're thinking, "Oh, I know what this is about," or "Here's what you should do." They keep it to themselves. A question from them at the right moment can help you hear your own Guide, and what you answer is up to you. Then you trade places, and it's your turn to keep "Here's what you should do" to yourself.

<!-- Turn 70: Joel's P5, word for word (his answer to question 44: "Just a little loosening of the march is what it needed. this is human"; experiments/t70-answers/JOEL-20261017-2259.md). His check counts for it alone. -->

The peer doesn't become your permanent parent, though. They show you a capacity, help you get the hang of it, and give it back to you, which is borrowed adulthood in its healthiest form (a driving instructor isn't planning to drive you around forever either). Is that therapy? Well, not the mainstream kind, but yes, for sure. Professional therapy's still useful for what it does, and peers and community do something else, so a serious healing path can include both.

### The Love Has to Move

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E31 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e31 a") -->

Community can come before the big opening or after it, too. Sometimes loving people give somebody enough safety and belonging that a deeper opening becomes possible. Other times it's the other way around. Somebody has a big opening first, a love or a meaning they couldn't have imagined before, and then they start to "manifest" different kinds of people and relationships in their life. Either way, the community is what helps make it practical, real, down to earth, embodied and so on.

<!-- Turn 69 (E188, E189), my own draft (experiments/t69-guide/drafts/st6b-a.txt): his published "This is part of why I connect inner-child pl/ork with Hearthwork and with building community." (question 43, 4: "my words"), then the loop. Alone Human Written (0.0098, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/613b1e94-cc35-46dd-80e8-4b0cd3dd20b6. The h1 with every paragraph read Human Written (0.13, high, 797 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/f38ec8b9-4655-4c2a-b918-991dad5e6c99 (round t70c), once P4's last sentence was changed (round t70b's h1 read Mostly Human, an AI window from P4's "Then you trade places." into his P5). -->

This is part of why I connect inner-child pl/ork with Hearthwork and with building community. Having a beautiful experience on the inside isn't really the point. The love has to move, and it doesn't always go the same way round (the door you came in through matters less than the loop). From a spiritual experience to your little one, then into your relationship with the grown-up you, then out to other people, and back again.

# How to Forgive Without Forgetting

<!-- Turn 66 (E180, E185), my own draft, joined: the first half of my w10, which read Human with the heading while its end read AI, and a new second half (round b). For many people healing eventually includes some forgiveness or release from resentment; it can't be scheduled, and safety, boundaries, accountability, justice and recovery don't wait for it; active resentment narrows what you see and keeps your little one organized around the injury; pressure to forgive early gets an enlightened-looking act while the wound goes underground. Alone Human Written (0.061, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/411ef235-3820-466d-8247-5064eff54143; with the heading Human (0.021, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/a76146bf-fab8-48a0-a9aa-e92713409277; the section, as installed, Human Written (0.028, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/a3f6ae6c-2ce3-4ff3-b4e7-5b1d6c04433f. Twenty-eight P1s read AI before it (turns 65 and 66). Record: experiments/t66-guide/REVIEWS.md. -->

For many people, healing does get around to some forgiveness eventually, or at least to not being run by the resentment anymore. It can't be put on a schedule (nobody gets a gold star for forgiving fastest). And you don't have to forgive first to get safe, set boundaries, hold someone accountable, get justice or get better. Then again, active resentment narrows what you see, and your little one stays organized around the injury. And if someone pressures you into forgiving early, what you get is an enlightened-looking act, with the wound gone underground.

The understanding that helps me is seeing how a person's conditioning, history, pain, and capacity shaped the harmful move they made. Picture everything that had made them who they were by that moment, what happened to them and what they learned, the pain they carried and what they could and couldn't handle. From in there, it was the only move they had. Hurt people often pass on pain they never worked through. You may have heard that from somebody telling you to forgive. When you see it for yourself, though, you might find yourself forgiving them, and if you've held them uniquely to blame, the rage can soften into something more accurate.

<!-- Turn 73 (his answers of 2026-10-18 21:58 UTC (experiments/t73-answers/JOEL-20261018-2158.md)): 55, 14, A ("remove that clause it is just confusing"): "and in theory they could have done something else" is out. experiments/t73-guide/drafts/c55-14.txt. Alone Human Written (0.21, high), https://www.pangram.com/history/ac25bc6d-2e5c-4f30-a38d-b9cce909f093; How to Forgive Without Forgetting with it Human Written (0.082, high, 504 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/3ac748b7-a43f-4904-8861-ff5ecf566c98 (round t73a). -->

They still have to answer for what they did, and it still hurt. Seeing how they got there doesn't make it okay. It's just that punishing them now can't reach back and give them what they were missing then, the sense to see it or the help. Or the skills. What's left is fixing what can be fixed, and making it easier for something different to happen now. So a boundary can still be right. So can the law, if it comes to that, or telling them plainly it was wrong (or telling yourself, if they'd never hear it). Keeping away from them for good can be right too, and you can still forgive them from over there. Understanding somebody's limitations should make my expectations more realistic, not make those limitations less consequential. "I understand how you became this way" can sit right next to "I now understand why I shouldn't rely on you for this."

Forgiving yourself works the same way, with the grounding's MUST (GROUNDING-fg01.md [17]): own only what was yours, feel the remorse, repair what you can, then stop holding yourself hostage while the responsibility stays; if that's hard, forgiving others tends to be harder too; some harms take years; forgiving too soon and living in resentment are the two traps (the guide's own repeat of P1's point, with the other trap added); come back now and then to how whoever did it became capable of it. Alone Human Written (0.066, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/1a3add5b-e0e3-46ea-a554-c33cebc1d874 (w5 before the fix 0.096). -->

## Forgiving Yourself

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E32 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e32 a") -->

Forgiving yourself works the same way. Own what you did (only what was yours), and let yourself feel the remorse. Repair what you can, by apologizing or making amends. Changing the behavior counts too. Then you're allowed to stop holding yourself hostage, and the responsibility stays. If you're thinking "not me, not after what I did," forgiving other people tends to be harder too. Every so often, come back to how whoever did it got to be someone who could do that, until you understand it well enough that it changes how you feel.

# When One Life Has Ended and the Next Hasn’t Begun

<!-- Turn 65 (E177), my own draft (le1-p1-c; GROUNDING-le01.md: "staying in one on new terms" for the guide's "on different terms"): your little one gets loud in the gap after one life ends; the bardo, without its teaching about death; endings leave a stretch with no old you and no new one. Alone Human Written (0.16), https://www.pangram.com/history/73ad8339-0cc7-4ce1-8f0d-33bdc2cd1baf. -->

The same list, the helper stepping back once you've grown (77.6, as a small scene), the stretch with no old you and no new one, and the bardo with its teachings left to you, without the chain of expository steps. Alone 100% Human, https://www.pangram.com/history/50f027e5-f84e-47d9-bbc0-43ee045bea9c; the h1's heading, P1, P2 and P3 100% Human, https://www.pangram.com/history/2e42b03b-65f0-4caf-955c-4c6863925169. -->

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 98.11 A (his "98. agree cut all"): cut "(I'm only borrowing the word, so what it teaches about death and rebirth is up to you)". The original is in experiments/t102-safety/SAFETY-CUTS.md. -->

Your little one can get really loud in the gap after one life ends and before the next one has formed. A breakup will do it, or moving to another country, an illness, losing your faith or a role, your youngest moving out. Maybe you've left a family or community, maybe you've stayed on new terms. A therapist or helper might step back because you're doing so much better, and suddenly your little one is wailing. For a while the old you is gone and the new you hasn't shown up yet. Tibetan Buddhism calls an in-between state like that a bardo.

<!-- Turn 67 (E186), my own draft (experiments/t67-guide/drafts/le2-f1c.txt): the first half of the guide's P2, its questions, with the grounding's "what actually ended" (unknown isn't the same as gone; ask your little one what they fear). Alone Human Written (0.34), https://www.pangram.com/history/387b3d6d-c325-44fa-b20e-a297796cf3f4. -->

Your little one might be sure everything's over. Is it? Usually one real thing ended, and there's other stuff you're still trying to keep going, maybe on autopilot. Some of it just isn't known yet, and unknown isn't the same as gone. Ask your little one what they're scared will happen in the meantime (worst-case scenarios are kind of their specialty).

<!-- Turn 68: Joel's second half of P2, word for word (2026-10-17 16:58 UTC, his answer to question 41: "I prefer this version which looks even more human to me and more inside the thought"; experiments/t68-answers/JOEL-20261017-1658.md), with "feels too heavy" for his "feels gets too heavy" (two verbs, one left from an edit; his rule: obvious typos get fixed, and "gets" is a word away if he meant that). Alone Human Written (0.018), https://www.pangram.com/history/b87eeae7-7b5c-4e20-b78d-d922437782f2. -->

Something basic can mark the ending, like boxing up the old keys. Nobody needs to know what door will open next. But when the not-knowing feels too heavy, a new identity can sure look tempting. Like a borrowed coat that doesn't fit but at least it's warm. But you can't go far with a borrowed coat anyway, so don't force the future on. Keep taking care of yourself, and grieve what ended with your little one beside you during the metamorphosis.

<!-- Turn 67 (E186), my own draft (drafts/le3a-n.txt): the missing-someone practice, the guide's P3 first half (the ache as a cue for love; "I miss you" as "I love you", or the loving-kindness wish; picture them or feel the direction of the care). Alone Human Written (0.56, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/eaaee8e8-0e7b-4072-a6a2-c9595ea26685. -->

## Missing Someone

<!-- Turn 106 (his answers of 2026-10-24 22:55 UTC, experiments/t106-answers/JOEL-20261024-2255.md): E33 from the ideas page, the heading only (his "e33 a") -->

When you miss someone, let the ache be a cue for love. It doesn't have to become one more problem to solve, and pushing it away won't help. If love's there, "I miss you" becomes "I love you." Or there's the loving-kindness wish: "May you be safe. May you be held in love." You can picture them. Or don't, and just feel the care going their way.

<!-- Turn 67 (E186), my own draft (drafts/le3b-a.txt): its limits (it happens in you; no stand-in for grieving, contact, boundaries or the practical things; simpler if it gets compulsive or unreal) and the grounding's MISSING care inward. Alone Human Written (0.038), https://www.pangram.com/history/50cdeb27-ee23-4e22-b6ae-00860e99a4c4. The h1 with every paragraph read Human Written (0.20, high, 435 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/aa326b66-3952-48f9-b535-d72e33e5d960 (round t68a). -->

<!-- Turn 103 (his answers of 2026-10-23 21:52 UTC, experiments/t103-answers/JOEL-20261023-2152.md): 97.15 A (his "97. i agree cut all"): cut "It doesn't stand in for grieving, though, or for getting in touch when that's right, and your boundaries still apply. Same with the practical stuff.". The original is in experiments/t102-safety/SAFETY-CUTS.md. -->

All of this happens in you, so it doesn't matter whether they get any of it (nobody's checking their inbox). And if it starts making you more compulsive, or a bit unreal, so you can't get back to your life, make it simpler: "I miss you, and I care." Some of that same care can go to the part of you that's still here.

# Finding a Therapist to Borrow Adulthood

It replaces the Appendix's three therapist paragraphs: How This Relates to Schema Therapy P3 (turn 73), How This Relates to IFS P8 (turn 71) and Teachers and Practice Resources P3 (his of 2026-10-18). The h1 Human Written (0.00035, high, 401 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/ea67f223-3c99-4e95-8488-5deb0edda3ef; his P2 to P5 alone Human Written (0.00003, 0.0005, 0.024, 0.0004), https://www.pangram.com/history/8d2e43fa-b35d-4751-96ad-c5a7628a0996, https://www.pangram.com/history/6a73cef6-c574-4a0f-89d6-b3416aaa7ae3, https://www.pangram.com/history/143a580e-0fb3-4039-af1e-75815d924ea7, https://www.pangram.com/history/057ab8e4-3e07-4164-9fc5-1c2b0f794c71; the Appendix without the three paragraphs Human Written (0.12, high, 1119 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/a7141598-29af-45ee-87e4-1c20a5a3197d (round t74a). -->

We all need some support, especially when taking our first steps. First and easiest is to start with finding a somatic therapist, if that resonates, as per my [Somatic Therapies Guide](http://somatic.u-dont-exist.com/).

Inner Child Reparenting therapists do exist, and you can find them, although they often are not licensed, and I don't know of any central directory, nor of any protocol they'd have in common. There are none which work with my guide yet. I can consult, and if you like the guide, you can ask a therapist to check it out and see what they agree with or disagree with as a great way to vet them.

The best licensed, listed therapists you'll find that come close will be doing Internal Family Systems (IFS), or Schema Therapy. I'll talk more about those in the appendix, but Schema Therapy comes closest to my approach. [Open Path Collective](https://openpathcollective.org/pricing-and-eligibility-for-affordable-therapy/) (or a similar resource in your country) lists thousands of affordable therapists. You can filter them by Schema or IFS. You can often find [even more Schema therapists at the official site](https://schematherapysociety.org/Search?&tab=1).

<!-- Turn 75 (his answer of 2026-10-19 00:26 UTC (experiments/t75-answers/JOEL-20261019-0026.md)): 63, A: one sentence of mine added to his P4, after his spiritual-experiences sentence, for his guide's safety points that the removed IFS P8 carried ("a skilled, grounded, trauma-informed IFS therapist who isn't suggestive may help, especially one who understands dissociation"). The rest is his, word for word. experiments/t75-guide/drafts/fat4-b.txt. Alone Human Written (0.022, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/f41601f7-d9bc-4575-a731-1baaf677279f; Finding a Therapist to Borrow Adulthood with it Human Written (0.00037, high, 418 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/4aa96771-0baf-4f97-8d71-838af7500b2f (round t75a). -->

Before choosing a therapist, get to know the strengths and pitfalls of their approach, including any modifications they make to the standard protocol. I'd especially want to interview them regarding how they treat spiritual experiences. You want someone who won't tell you what yours really mean, downplay them, force them, or treat them as a symptom. I'd also want one who doesn't put ideas in your head, and who understands dissociation. What have they found too challenging to deal with so far, and where have they seen the best results? A good therapist won't charge you an arm and a leg just for this kind of interview, and they should already have this information on their website to avoid wasting too much time. A certificate on the wall can't tell you any of that.

Regarding AI therapy, I've tried all the top AI therapy apps, and they're all pretty bad. I'm trying to make my own based on this guide, but I'm becoming more convinced that unsupervised AI therapy is just a toss up. That doesn't mean you can't get a lot of great insights sometimes when talking to AI, though, but just expect it to go sideways sometimes.

# You Are Worthy of Love. Always.

But love from another person ... obviously is more conditional than that. so the ending might need a bit more clarity"), waiting for this section since then; turn 65's closing below left out the guide's "Love precedes anything you could earn. Alone Human Written (0.00044), https://www.pangram.com/history/f89caf0e-4e7a-4afd-ba75-0ce25aac72fd; the h1 with it Human Written (0.031, high, 152 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/93f491d2-249d-4375-8776-ab6b12445e7e (round t71mmm). It's the same love as back in Your Loan is PRE-APPROVED!, the Divine Love that can start as a little spark of … something when you give thanks. That kind, whether you'd say it comes from God or the universe itself or whatever, was there before you could earn anything. Love from a person is more conditional than that. Somebody's love running out on you still says nothing about what you're worth.

<!-- Turn 65 (E177), round z, fresh writer 1 (job wl1): the guide's closing, minus what the article already says (start with small acts of care; the role can start outside you; love before trust; your little one is still in there): be patient with yourself, and gentle; you are more than your pain and your past; you aren't alone, and help exists. Alone Human Written (0.019, low), https://www.pangram.com/history/c3e54ace-c815-470d-b4a6-9f51bc740360; with its heading Human Written (0.21), https://www.pangram.com/history/e7251972-1046-44b9-875d-1be4745e71e4. Cold read OK (experiments/t65-guide/COLD-READ-wl-1.md). -->

Be patient with yourself, and gentle, including with the part of you that thinks you should be over it by now. You are more than your pain and your past. That's easy to lose sight of when you've spent so long looking at them. And you aren't alone. There's help, and lots of other people are out there learning to look after their own little ones.

# Appendix

## How This Relates to Schema Therapy

<!-- Turn 72 (his answers of 2026-10-18 19:06 UTC (experiments/t72-answers/JOEL-20261018-1906.md)): as st-3, with its first paragraph only. experiments/t72-guide/drafts/st-c.txt. Its second paragraph, on the differences, read AI alone in all three versions (rounds t72a to t72c), so the section is this paragraph for now. -->

<!-- Turn 87 (his answers of 2026-10-22 15:33 UTC (experiments/t87-answers/JOEL-20261022-1533.md)): 77.8, his correction ("setting limits is ... more of a protector job than a guide job"): the Healthy Adult also sets limits and "gets on with adult life", linked to Young's institute's mode list ("performs appropriate adult functions such as working, parenting, taking responsibility, and committing"), so P1 now names all three jobs. Lint REVIEW at most; not yet checked in Pangram. Drafts: experiments/t87-drafts/. -->

Of the clinical approaches I know, schema therapy is the closest to this guide. Its therapists do what they call [limited reparenting](https://schematherapysociety.org/Limited-Reparenting): within professional limits, they give you some of what a good parent would, with warmth and firmness and some playfulness too. Over time you take that in, and it becomes your own "Healthy Adult," who looks after your "Vulnerable Child" and stands up to the punishing parent in your head. It sets limits, too. And it gets on with [adult life](https://schematherapy.com/id72.htm), like work and the things you've committed to. Sound familiar? That's a Nurturer, a Protector and a Guide by other names, and since the therapist's version becomes yours bit by bit, it's borrowed adulthood too.

Where it really differs is spirituality. Schema therapy doesn't build it in, though the preface to its main textbook calls blending it with meditation or religious practice an important development. That's not so surprising, since it grew out of cognitive therapy. Some people do blend them, though. Tara Bennett-Goleman's book *Emotional Alchemy*, for one, fits a Buddhist loving-kindness wish to each schema. Her book barely mentions gratitude, and for me that's the door to the bigger love back in Your Loan is PRE-APPROVED! IFS goes overboard the other way, in my opinion (more on that in the next section).

## How This Relates to IFS

<!-- Turn 71 (the overnight run): the guide's overlap (IFS recognizes protective parts and teaches people to approach them without shame or combat, so "meet the protector first" fits), opening on a pointer of mine to Richard Schwartz's No Bad Parts on the IFS Institute's own page, https://ifs-institute.com/store/419 (opened 2026-10-18), with his "honor, understand, and love every part" from that page, unquoted (not in the guide, so question 50 asks whether he wants it). Thirteen versions of the section read AI before tonight's sources (rounds t71d to t71aaa). experiments/t71-guide/drafts/ifs1-x.txt. Alone Human Written (0.010), https://www.pangram.com/history/e57d9c28-ddd6-4754-bcb7-cc4dcf0552f2 (round t71aaa); the h2 below. -->

Richard Schwartz, who developed Internal Family Systems (IFS), called one of his books [No Bad Parts](https://ifs-institute.com/store/419). Honor, understand and love every part, he says, and the protective ones are no exception, so "meet the protector first" would fit right in over there. That's a lot of the overlap with this method, right there in the title.

<!-- Turn 71: his two first-person lines as his published guide has them, "My reparenting method diverges from IFS at an important point." and "I emphasize the inner adult you build and practice occupying: Nurturer, Protector, and Leader, Guide, or Guru." (his story; their wording waits on his word, E189, question 45; OWNER-EDITS appendix-first-person-pending-20261018), with the guide's standard IFS between them in the IFS Institute's own words from its outline of the model (opened 2026-10-18: "everyone has a Self", the Self "can and should lead", parts that "blend" with it), and a pointer back to The Chicken-and-Egg Problem's "one grown-up doing three jobs". experiments/t71-guide/drafts/ifs2-u.txt. Alone Human Written (0.011), https://www.pangram.com/history/98682bd9-2204-4e81-9a26-4142af90fb53 (round t71ww). -->

My reparenting method diverges from IFS at an important point. The IFS Institute's [outline of the model](https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/internal-family-systems-model-outline) says "everyone has a Self," and that the Self "can and should lead," once your parts stop "blending" with it. I emphasize the inner adult you build and practice occupying: Nurturer, Protector, and Leader, Guide, or Guru. That's still one grown-up with three jobs, like back in The Chicken-and-Egg Problem.

Can't find a calm, compassionate Self in there? Then borrow. One grown-up job at a time, from a friend who'll play Protector for a night, or from something you do, like letting the phone ring when you know how that call goes (The Parent You Inherited again), until it's yours.

Is a perfect Higher Self already waiting in there to be your guide or parent? I'd call that claim [spiritual bypassing](https://goenka.u-dont-exist.com/). I've had the Higher Self perspective myself, though. It's a somewhat less subjective way of seeing things as working perfectly in reality, despite the ego's illusions, which matches what people say about it. Where I part ways with IFS is the wisdom part. I don't think the Higher Self has any specific wisdom. It's a vantage point, where more wisdom and compassion can come up, but problems can also look like they were never problems at all from up there, and that can ironically lead instead to more foolish indifference.

You can see both sides of this see-saw in the guru types who supposedly embody their Higher Self. [Anthony de Mello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_de_Mello_(Jesuit_priest)) is an excellent example. He was a Jesuit priest who mixed stories from all kinds of traditions into his teaching on seeing through the ego's traps and coming back to the Perfect Present. He also had a background as a psychotherapist who understood trauma-informed healing methods. He seemed to drift between the two opposite paradigms in a way that contradicted itself. And that bizarre contradiction is itself cited as "crazy wisdom" by apologists.

Alone Human Written (0.18, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/3a066c40-ec17-4ce1-a5ac-a2fbda6fe580; the Appendix with it Human Written (0.098, high, 1331 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/c59b7508-347a-4eab-a68b-7bad6c3d2229 (round t72f). -->

So inner child therapy draws on those higher vantage points more concretely. The inner Guide is the closest thing here to a Higher Self, and it has to work with the Nurturer and Protector and come after them. It can't act like some transcendent authority that waves everything away. Most reparenting doesn't do enough with the Guide, which is the opposite extreme from IFS, so I'm trying to bring together the best of both. IFS isn't exactly one system, so I may be simplifying it here. It has its own branches and disputes, and that's one more problem I'm trying to remedy by laying all this out systematically in this guide.

IFS calls these wounded young parts “exiles.” I find that pathologizing. I prefer to speak of a child who needs parenting and integration, rather than naming them by their wounded or excluded condition.

And the parts just keep coming: managers, firefighters, exiles, polarized parts, legacy burdens, Self-energy, guides, new subparts... At some point it’s a maze, and as my friend Pink, who has been healing her Dissociative Identity Disorder, explained, while it can be useful to have validation of the parts that one experiences, and ideas for how to work with them, it can also get overwhelming. People who are already overwhelmed can thus become more split up than before. I've seen many others echo this same evaluation, and it makes sense to me.

This framework developed through the teachers and collaborators credited below and my own practice. Later revisions also compare notes with schema therapy, compassion-focused therapy, attachment repair, motivational interviewing, mentalizing, metacognitive therapy, behavioral activation, radically open DBT, emotion-focused therapy and spiritually integrated psychotherapy. I borrow particular ideas and practices from each of them, but the mix is mine, so please don't blame them for it.

Daja works with inner-child pl/ork intuitively, through the divine Nurturer and Protector archetypes. Her work is less neuroscience and CBT and more direct archetypal practice, and the three grown-up jobs draw a lot from it. She has a [book](https://a.co/d/7Sa4kP2) and a [free one-hour YouTube training](https://youtube.com/playlist?list=PL_kbcFWhZK0wJeFUShGvHFZa1u3x7p8Y-&si=Fr0g76mDh8-WLMjM), and she's on [Instagram](http://instagram.com/ihopeyoufindserenity).

Alone Human Written (0.24, medium), https://www.pangram.com/history/48efac73-5baa-433a-bf91-37a59d2fc3ea; the h2 Human Written (0.13, high, 270 words), https://www.pangram.com/history/e8c37784-487f-4de7-96d8-6d72844c30a9 (round t71g). -->

Lisa Romano brings more neuroscience and CBT, and her [video](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2tbMRZKdHJ4) is a good place to start. She makes the point that remembering the specific details of childhood trauma isn't necessary, because the felt body knows what needs to come up. [Key](https://open.substack.com/users/432335983-key-to-enlightenment?utm_source=mentions) developed the Guide's sense of direction, and the identity and differentiation work from Bowen I mentioned earlier. She led a [guided inner-child meditation](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UkVlDixqqA0) in Nairobi in January 2026 for twenty people, and it's up on YouTube.
